# Conservatism

"Conservatives" redirects here. For specific political parties, see [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(disambiguation\)).

This article is about conservatism as a political and social philosophy. For other uses of conservatism and conservative, see [Conservatism (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_\(disambiguation\)).

Not to be confused with [Conservation movement](45441.md).

**Conservatism** is a [cultural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy_of_culture), [social](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_philosophy), and [political philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_philosophy) and [ideology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideology) that seeks to promote and preserve [traditional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tradition) [institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institution), [customs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_\(norm\)), and [values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Value_\(ethics_and_social_sciences\)).[^cite_note-\:1-1][^cite_note-2][^cite_note-3] The central tenets of conservatism may vary in relation to the [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture) and [civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civilization) in which it appears.[^cite_note-\:6-4] In [Western culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture), depending on the particular nation and the particular time period, conservatives seek to promote and preserve a range of institutions, such as the [nuclear family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_family), [organized religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organized_religion), the [military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military), the [nation-state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation-state), [constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution), [property rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Property_rights), [rule of law](25166191.md), [aristocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristocracy), and [monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy).[^cite_note-5][^cite_note-6]

The 18th-century Anglo-Irish statesman [Edmund Burke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Burke), who opposed the [French Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution), is credited as one of the forefathers of Western conservative thought in the 1790s along with Savoyard statesman [Joseph de Maistre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_de_Maistre).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFawcett20203–18-7] The first established use of the term in a political context originated in 1818 with [François-René de Chateaubriand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois-Ren%C3%A9_de_Chateaubriand) during the period of [Bourbon Restoration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourbon_Restoration_in_France) that sought to roll back the policies of the French Revolution and establish social order.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMuller199726-8]

Conservatism has varied considerably as it has adapted itself to existing traditions and national cultures.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeywood201763-9] Thus, conservatives from different parts of the world, each upholding their respective traditions, may disagree on a wide range of issues.[^cite_note-10] One of the three major ideologies along with [liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism) and [socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENisbet200215-11] conservatism is the dominant ideology in many countries across the world, including [Hungary](#Hungary), [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_India), [Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_Iran), [Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_Israel),[^cite_note-\:13-12] [Italy](#Italy), [Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_Japan), [Poland](#Poland), and [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_Russia). Historically associated with [right-wing politics](56522.md), the term has been used to describe [a wide range of views](#Ideological_variants). Conservatism may be either [libertarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libertarian_conservatism) or [authoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian_conservatism),[^cite_note-13] [populist](8354382.md) or [elitist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elitism),[^cite_note-14] [progressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_conservatism) or [reactionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactionary),[^cite_note-15] [moderate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moderate_conservatism) or [extreme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultraconservatism).[^cite_note-16]

## Beliefs and principles

Scholars have tried to define conservatism as a set of beliefs or principles. Political scientist [Andrew Heywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Heywood) argues that the five central beliefs of conservatism are tradition, human imperfection, organic society, authority/hierarchy, and property.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeywood201765–73-17] Historian [Russell Kirk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russell_Kirk) developed five canons of conservatism in *[The Conservative Mind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Conservative_Mind)* (1953):

- A belief in a transcendent order, which Kirk described variously as based in tradition, [divine revelation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_revelation), or [natural law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_law);

- An affection for the "variety and mystery" of human existence;

- A conviction that society requires orders and classes that emphasize natural distinctions;

- A belief that [property](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Property) and [freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom) are closely linked;

- A faith in custom, [convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_\(norm\)), and prescription, and a recognition that innovation must be tied to existing traditions and customs, which entails a respect for the political value of [prudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prudence).

  [^cite_note-18]

Some political scientists, such as [Samuel P. Huntington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_P._Huntington), have added that while conservatism has core values, going back to the teachings of [Edmund Burke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Burke), it is also a situational ideology, which aims to conserve diverse social traditions.[^cite_note-Huntington456457-19] Under this definition, conservatives are seen as defending the established institutions of their time.[^cite_note-20] According to [Quintin Hogg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quintin_Hogg,_Baron_Hailsham_of_St_Marylebone), the chairman of the British [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)) in 1959: "Conservatism is not so much a philosophy as an attitude, a constant force, performing a timeless function in the development of a free society, and corresponding to a deep and permanent requirement of human nature itself."[^cite_note-21]

Conservatism has been called a "philosophy of human imperfection" by political scientist [Noël O'Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No%C3%ABl_O'Sullivan), reflecting among its adherents a negative view of [human nature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_nature) and pessimism of the potential to improve it through 'utopian' schemes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeywood201767-22] Political philosopher [Thomas Hobbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Hobbes), the "intellectual godfather of the realist right", argued that the [state of nature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_nature) for humans was "poor, nasty, brutish, and short", requiring centralized authority with royal [sovereignty](28791.md) to guarantee [law and order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_and_order_\(politics\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFawcett202048-23] Political philosopher [Edmund Burke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Burke), often called the father of modern conservatism, believed that human beings are steeped in [original sin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Original_sin) and that society therefore needs traditional institutions, such as an [established church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Established_church) and a landed [aristocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristocracy), in order to function.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEncyclopædia_Britannica-24]

### Tradition

Despite the lack of a universal definition, certain themes can be recognized as common across conservative thought. According to philosopher [Michael Oakeshott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Oakeshott):

> To be conservative \[…] is to prefer the familiar to the unknown, to prefer the tried to the untried, fact to mystery, the actual to the possible, the limited to the unbounded, the near to the distant, the sufficient to the superabundant, the convenient to the perfect, present laughter to utopian bliss.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeywood2004346-25]

Such traditionalism may be a reflection of trust in time-tested methods of social organization, giving 'votes to the dead'.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeywood201766-26] Traditions may also be steeped in a sense of [identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Identity_\(social_science\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeywood201766-26]

### Hierarchy

In contrast to the tradition-based definition of conservatism, some left-wing political theorists like [Corey Robin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corey_Robin) define conservatism primarily in terms of a general defense of [social](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_inequality) and [economic inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_inequality).[^cite_note-27] From this perspective, conservatism is less an attempt to uphold old institutions and more "a meditation on—and theoretical rendition of—the felt experience of having power, seeing it threatened, and trying to win it back".[^cite_note-28]

In *[Conservatism: A Rediscovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism:_A_Rediscovery)* (2022), political philosopher [Yoram Hazony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoram_Hazony) argues that, in a traditional conservative community, members have importance and influence to the degree they are [honored](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honor) within the social hierarchy, which includes factors such as age, experience, and wisdom.[^cite_note-29] Conservatives often glorify hierarchies, as demonstrated in an aphorism by conservative philosopher [Nicolás Gómez Dávila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicol%C3%A1s_G%C3%B3mez_D%C3%A1vila): "Hierarchies are celestial. In hell all are equal."[^cite_note-30] The word hierarchy has religious roots and translates to 'rule of a high priest.'[^cite_note-31]

### Authority

Authority is a core tenet of conservatism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGiubilei201918–19-32][^cite_note-33][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeywood201772-34] More specifically, conservatives tend to believe in [traditional authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_authority). According to sociologist [Max Weber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Weber), this form of authority is "resting on an established belief in the [sanctity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacredness) of immemorial traditions and the legitimacy of those exercising authority under them".[^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36] Philosopher [Alexandre Kojève](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandre_Koj%C3%A8ve) distinguishes between two different forms of traditional authority:

- The Authority of the Father—represented by actual fathers as well as conceptual fathers such as priests and monarchs.

- The Authority of the Master—represented by aristocrats and military commanders.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKojève202014–28-37]

Sociologist [Robert Nisbet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Nisbet) acknowledges that the decline of traditional authority in the modern world is partly linked with the retreat of old institutions such as [guild](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_guild), [order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fraternal_order), [parish](23623.md), and [family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family)—institutions that formerly acted as intermediaries between the state and the individual.[^cite_note-38][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKojève2020xvii-39] Philosopher [Hannah Arendt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hannah_Arendt) argues that the modern world suffers an existential crisis with a "dramatic breakdown of all traditional authorities," which are needed for the continuity of an established civilization.[^cite_note-40][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKojève2020xviii–xix-41]

## Historical background

Anglo-Irish statesman [Edmund Burke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Burke) has been widely regarded as the philosophical founder of modern conservatism.[^cite_note-42][^cite_note-43] He served as the private secretary to the [Marquess of Rockingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Watson-Wentworth,_2nd_Marquess_of_Rockingham) and as official pamphleteer to the [Rockingham branch of the Whig party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockingham_Whigs).[^cite_note-44] Together with the Tories, they were the conservatives in the late 18th century United Kingdom.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAuerbach195933-45]



[Edmund Burke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Burke), widely regarded as the philosophical founder of modern conservatism

Burke's views were a mixture of conservatism and republicanism. He supported the [American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution) of 1775–1783 but abhorred the violence of the [French Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution) of 1789–1799. He accepted the ideals of [private property](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_property) and the economics of [Adam Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Smith), but he thought that [capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitalism) should remain subordinate to the conservative social ethic and that the business class should be subordinate to aristocracy.[^cite_note-46] He insisted on standards of [honor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honor) derived from the medieval aristocratic tradition and saw the aristocracy as the nation's natural leaders.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAuerbach195937–40-47] That meant limits on the powers of the Crown, since he found the institutions of Parliament to be better informed than commissions appointed by the executive. He favored an established church, but allowed for a degree of [religious toleration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_toleration).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAuerbach195952–54-48] Burke ultimately justified the social order on the basis of tradition: tradition represented the wisdom of the species, and he valued community and social harmony over social reforms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAuerbach195941-49]



[Joseph de Maistre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_de_Maistre), a French philosopher and proponent of the [Counter-Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-Enlightenment)

Another form of conservatism developed in France in parallel to conservatism in Britain. It was influenced by [Counter-Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-Enlightenment) works by philosophers such as [Joseph de Maistre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_de_Maistre) and [Louis de Bonald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_de_Bonald).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeill202138–43-50] Many continental conservatives do not support [separation of church and state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separation_of_church_and_state), with most supporting state cooperation with the [Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_France), such as had existed in France before the Revolution. Conservatives were also early to embrace [nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalism), which was previously associated with liberalism and the Revolution in France.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdams200146-51] Another early French conservative, [François-René de Chateaubriand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois-Ren%C3%A9_de_Chateaubriand), espoused a [romantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism) opposition to modernity, contrasting its emptiness with the 'full heart' of traditional faith and loyalty.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFawcett202020-52] Elsewhere on the continent, German thinkers [Justus Möser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justus_M%C3%B6ser) and [Friedrich von Gentz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_von_Gentz) criticized the [Declaration of the Rights of Man and of the Citizen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_the_Rights_of_Man_and_of_the_Citizen) that came of the Revolution. Opposition was also expressed by [German idealists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_idealism) such as [Adam Müller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_M%C3%BCller) and [Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georg_Wilhelm_Friedrich_Hegel), the latter inspiring both leftist and rightist followers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFawcett202025–30-53]

Both Burke and Maistre were critical of [democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy) in general, though their reasons differed. Maistre was pessimistic about humans being able to follow rules, while Burke was skeptical about humans' innate ability to make rules. For Maistre, rules had a divine origin, while Burke believed they arose from custom. The lack of custom for Burke, and the lack of divine guidance for Maistre, meant that people would act in terrible ways. Both also believed that liberty of the wrong kind led to bewilderment and political breakdown. Their ideas would together flow into a stream of anti-rationalist, romantic conservatism, but would still stay separate. Burke was more open to argumentation and disagreement, while Maistre sought faith and authority, leading to a more illiberal strain of thought.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFawcett20205–7-54]

## Ideological variants

### Authoritarian conservatism

Main article: [Authoritarian conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian_conservatism)

See also: [Far-right politics](221220.md) and [Ultraconservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultraconservatism)

Authoritarian conservatism refers to [autocratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocratic) regimes that portray authority as absolute and unquestionable.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeywood201772-34][^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56] Authoritarian conservative movements show strong devotion towards religion, tradition, and culture while also expressing fervent nationalism akin to other far-right nationalist movements.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreedenSargentStears2013294–297-57][^cite_note-58] Examples of authoritarian conservative dictators include Marshal [Philippe Pétain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippe_P%C3%A9tain) in France,[^cite_note-\:10-59] Regent [Miklós Horthy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikl%C3%B3s_Horthy) in Hungary,[^cite_note-\:8-60] General [Ioannis Metaxas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ioannis_Metaxas) in Greece,[^cite_note-61] King [Alexander I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_I_of_Yugoslavia) in Yugoslavia,[^cite_note-62] Prime Minister [António de Oliveira Salazar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ant%C3%B3nio_de_Oliveira_Salazar) in Portugal,[^cite_note-63] Chancellor [Engelbert Dollfuss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Engelbert_Dollfuss) in Austria,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBischof200326-64] *Generalissimo* [Francisco Franco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Franco) in Spain,[^cite_note-65] King [Carol II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carol_II_of_Romania) in Romania,[^cite_note-\:11-66] and Tsar [Boris III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_III_of_Bulgaria) in Bulgaria.[^cite_note-67]





King [Alexander I of Yugoslavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_I_of_Yugoslavia) and Chancellor [Engelbert Dollfuss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Engelbert_Dollfuss) of Austria, authoritarian conservative dictators who were assassinated by fascist and Nazi political enemies

Authoritarian conservative movements were prominent in the same era as [fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism), with which they sometimes clashed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlinkhorn199010-68] Although both ideologies shared core values such as nationalism and had common enemies such as [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism), there was nonetheless a contrast between the traditionalist and elitist nature of authoritarian conservatism and the revolutionary and populist nature of fascism—thus it was common for authoritarian conservative regimes to suppress rising fascist and [Nazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism) movements.[^cite_note-\:11-66] The hostility between the two ideologies is highlighted by the struggle for power in Austria, which was marked by the assassination of the ultra-Catholic dictator [Engelbert Dollfuss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Engelbert_Dollfuss) by [Austrian Nazis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrian_Nazism). Likewise, [Croatian fascists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Usta%C5%A1e) assassinated King [Alexander I of Yugoslavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_I_of_Yugoslavia).[^cite_note-69] In Romania, as the fascist [Iron Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Guard) was gaining popularity and [Nazi Germany](21212.md) was making advances on the European political stage, King [Carol II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carol_II_of_Romania) ordered the execution of [Corneliu Zelea Codreanu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corneliu_Zelea_Codreanu) and other top-ranking Romanian fascists.[^cite_note-70] The exiled German Emperor [Wilhelm II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilhelm_II) was an enemy of [Adolf Hitler](2731583.md) and stated that Nazism made him ashamed to be a German for the first time in his life.[^cite_note-\:7-71] The Catholic seminarian [António de Oliveira Salazar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ant%C3%B3nio_de_Oliveira_Salazar), who was Portugal's dictator for 40 years, denounced fascism and Nazism as a "pagan [Caesarism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesarism)" that did not recognize legal, religious, or moral limits.[^cite_note-72]

Political scientist [Seymour Martin Lipset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seymour_Martin_Lipset) has examined the class basis of right-wing extremist politics in the 1920–1960 era. He reports:

> Conservative or rightist extremist movements have arisen at different periods in modern history, ranging from the [Horthyites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikl%C3%B3s_Horthy) in Hungary, the [Christian Social Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Social_Party_\(Austria\)) of Dollfuss in Austria, *[Der Stahlhelm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Der_Stahlhelm,_Bund_der_Frontsoldaten)* and other nationalists in pre-Hitler Germany, and [Salazar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ant%C3%B3nio_de_Oliveira_Salazar) in Portugal, to the pre-1966 [Gaullist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaullism) movements and the monarchists in contemporary France and Italy. The right extremists are conservative, not revolutionary. They seek to change political institutions in order to preserve or restore cultural and economic ones, while [extremists of the centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extremism_of_the_centre) \[fascists/Nazis] and left \[communists/anarchists] seek to use political means for cultural and social revolution. The ideal of the right extremist is not a [totalitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totalitarianism) ruler, but a monarch, or a traditionalist who acts like one. Many such movements in Spain, Austria, Hungary, Germany, and Italy have been explicitly monarchist \[…] The supporters of these movements differ from those of the centrists, tending to be wealthier, and more religious, which is more important in terms of a potential for mass support.[^cite_note-73]

[Edmund Fawcett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Fawcett) states that fascism is totalitarian, populist, and anti-[pluralist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pluralism_\(political_theory\)), whereas authoritarian conservatism is somewhat pluralist but most of all elitist and anti-populist. He concludes: "The fascist is a nonconservative who takes [anti-liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-liberalism) to extremes. The right-wing authoritarian is a conservative who takes fear of democracy to extremes."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFawcett2020263-74]

During the [Cold War](325329.md), right-wing military dictatorships were prominent in Latin America, with most nations being under military rule by the middle of the 1970s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERemmer198910-75] One example of this was General [Augusto Pinochet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augusto_Pinochet), who ruled over Chile from 1973 to 1990.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERemmer19895–6-76] According to [Erik von Kuehnelt-Leddihn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erik_von_Kuehnelt-Leddihn), military dictatorships arise in democratic systems in order to stop leftist parties from becoming totalitarian.[^cite_note-\:12-77] The most recent instance occurred in Bolivia in 2024, when General [Juan José Zúñiga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Jos%C3%A9_Z%C3%BA%C3%B1iga) staged a coup in order to overthrow the far-left president [Luis Arce](38820569.md).[^cite_note-78]

In the 21st century, the authoritarian style of government experienced a worldwide renaissance with conservative politicians such as President [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) in Russia, President [Recep Tayyip Erdoğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan) in Turkey, Prime Minister [Viktor Orbán](750582.md) in Hungary, Prime Minister [Narendra Modi](444222.md) in India, and President [Donald Trump](4848272.md) in the United States.[^cite_note-79]

### Liberal conservatism

Main article: [Liberal conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_conservatism)

Not to be confused with [Conservative liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_liberalism).

Liberal conservatism is a variant of conservatism that is strongly influenced by liberal stances.[^cite_note-80] It incorporates the [classical liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_liberal) view of minimal [economic interventionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_interventionism), meaning that individuals should be free to participate in the market and generate wealth without government interference.[^cite_note-\:2-81] However, individuals cannot be thoroughly depended on to act responsibly in other spheres of life; therefore, liberal conservatives believe that a strong state is necessary to ensure law and order, and social institutions are needed to nurture a sense of [duty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duty) and responsibility to the nation.[^cite_note-\:2-81] Originally opposed to capitalism and the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGiubilei201921-82][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENisbet200228–31-83] the conservative ideology in many countries adopted [economic liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalism), especially in the United States where this ideology is known as [fiscal conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiscal_conservatism).[^cite_note-\:4-84][^cite_note-85]

### National conservatism

Main article: [National conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_conservatism)



[Giorgia Meloni](29228976.md)—leader of the national-conservative party [Brothers of Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brothers_of_Italy), the first female [Prime Minister of Italy](273741.md), and president of the [European Conservatives and Reformists Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Conservatives_and_Reformists_Party)

National conservatism prioritizes the defense of [national](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_identity) and [cultural identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_identity), often based on a theory of the [family as a model for the state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_as_a_model_for_the_state).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeywood201792-86] National conservatism is oriented towards upholding national [sovereignty](28791.md), which includes limited immigration and a strong national defense.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreedenSargentStears2013465–469-87] In Europe, national conservatives are usually [eurosceptics](10037.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeywood201790-88][^cite_note-89] Political philosopher [Yoram Hazony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoram_Hazony) has argued for national conservatism in his work *[The Virtue of Nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Virtue_of_Nationalism)* (2018).[^cite_note-90]

### Paternalistic conservatism

Main article: [Paternalistic conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paternalistic_conservatism)

Paternalistic conservatism is a strand in conservatism which reflects the belief that societies exist and develop organically and that members within them have obligations towards each other.[^cite_note-91] There is particular emphasis on the paternalistic obligation (*[noblesse oblige](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noblesse_oblige)*) of those who are privileged and wealthy to the poorer parts of society, which is consistent with principles such as [duty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duty), [organicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organic_unity), and [hierarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hierarchy).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeywood201776–77-92] Its proponents often stress the importance of a [social safety net](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_safety_net) to deal with poverty, supporting limited [redistribution of wealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redistribution_of_wealth) along with government regulation of markets in the interests of both consumers and producers.[^cite_note-93]

Paternalistic conservatism first arose as a distinct ideology in the [United Kingdom](31717.md) under Prime Minister [Benjamin Disraeli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Disraeli)'s "[One Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_nation_conservatism)" [Toryism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toryism).[^cite_note-94] There have been a variety of one-nation conservative governments in the United Kingdom with exponents such as Prime Ministers Disraeli, [Stanley Baldwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_Baldwin), [Neville Chamberlain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neville_Chamberlain), [Winston Churchill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston_Churchill), and [Harold Macmillan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harold_Macmillan).[^cite_note-95]

In 19th-century [Germany](11867.md), Chancellor [Otto von Bismarck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_von_Bismarck) adopted a set of social programs, known as [state socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Socialism_\(Germany\)), which included insurance for workers against sickness, accident, incapacity, and old age. The goal of this conservative state-building strategy was to make ordinary Germans, not just the [Junker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junker_\(Prussia\)) aristocracy, more loyal to state and [Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Emperor).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEncyclopædia_Britannica-24] Chancellor [Leo von Caprivi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leo_von_Caprivi) promoted a conservative agenda called the "New Course".[^cite_note-96]

### Progressive conservatism

Main article: [Progressive conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_conservatism)

In the United States, President [Theodore Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt) has been identified as the main exponent of progressive conservatism. Roosevelt stated that he had "always believed that wise progressivism and wise conservatism go hand in hand".[^cite_note-\:0-97] The [Republican](32070.md) administration of President [William Howard Taft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Howard_Taft) was progressive conservative, and he described himself as a believer in progressive conservatism.[^cite_note-\:0-97] President [Dwight D. Eisenhower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwight_D._Eisenhower) also declared himself an advocate of progressive conservatism.[^cite_note-98]

In [Canada](5042916.md), a variety of conservative governments have been part of the [Red Tory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Tory) tradition, with Canada's former major conservative party being named the [Progressive Conservative Party of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Party_of_Canada) from 1942 to 2003.[^cite_note-Hugh_Segal_2011._pp._113–148-99] Prime Ministers [Arthur Meighen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Meighen), [R. B. Bennett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R._B._Bennett), [John Diefenbaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Diefenbaker), [Joe Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Clark), [Brian Mulroney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Mulroney), and [Kim Campbell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Campbell) led Red Tory federal governments.[^cite_note-Hugh_Segal_2011._pp._113–148-99]

### Reactionary conservatism

Main article: [Reactionary conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactionary_conservatism)





Italian esotericist [Julius Evola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julius_Evola) and Colombian aphorist [Nicolás Gómez Dávila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicol%C3%A1s_G%C3%B3mez_D%C3%A1vila)—prominent reactionary critics of modernity

Reactionary conservatism, also known as reactionism, opposes policies for the [social transformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_transformation) of society.[^cite_note-100] In popular usage, reactionism refers to a staunch [traditionalist conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditionalist_conservative) political perspective of a person who supports the status quo and opposes social, political, and economic change.[^cite_note-101] Some adherents of conservatism, rather than opposing change, seek to return to the *[status quo ante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Status_quo)* and tend to view the modern world in a negative light, especially concerning [mass culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_culture) and [secularism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism), although different groups of reactionaries may choose different traditional values to revive.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEncyclopædia_Britannica-24][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcLeanMcMillan2009-102]

Some political scientists, such as [Corey Robin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corey_Robin), treat the words reactionary and conservative as synonyms.[^cite_note-103] Others, such as [Mark Lilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Lilla), argue that reactionism and conservatism are distinct worldviews.[^cite_note-104] [Francis Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Wilson_\(political_scientist\)) defines conservatism as "a philosophy of social evolution, in which certain lasting values are defended within the framework of the tension of political conflict".[^cite_note-105]

Some reactionaries favor a return to the *status quo ante*, the previous political state of society, which that person believes possessed positive characteristics absent from contemporary society. An early example of a powerful reactionary movement was [German Romanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Romanticism), which centered around concepts of organicism, [medievalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medievalism), and traditionalism against the forces of rationalism, secularism, and individualism that were unleashed in the [French Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution).[^cite_note-\:3-106]

In political discourse, being a reactionary is generally regarded as negative; Peter King observed that it is "an unsought-for label, used as a torment rather than a badge of honor".[^cite_note-107] Despite this, the descriptor has been adopted by intellectuals such as Italian esoteric traditionalist [Julius Evola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julius_Evola),[^cite_note-108] Austrian monarchist [Erik von Kuehnelt-Leddihn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erik_von_Kuehnelt-Leddihn),[^cite_note-109] Colombian [political theologian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_theology) [Nicolás Gómez Dávila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicol%C3%A1s_G%C3%B3mez_D%C3%A1vila), and American historian [John Lukacs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Lukacs).[^cite_note-110]

### Religious conservatism

See also: [Christian right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_right), [Hindutva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindutva), [Integralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integralism), [Islamism](15012.md), and [Religious Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_Zionism)

Religious conservatism principally applies the teachings of particular religions to politics—sometimes by merely proclaiming the value of those teachings, at other times by having those teachings influence laws.[^cite_note-111] In most democracies, political conservatism seeks to uphold traditional family structures and social values. Religious conservatives typically oppose abortion, [LGBT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT) behavior (or, in certain cases, identity), drug use,[^cite_note-112] and sexual activity outside of marriage. In some cases, conservative values are grounded in religious beliefs, and conservatives seek to increase the role of religion in public life.[^cite_note-113]

[Christian democracy](328969.md) is an ideology inspired by [Christian social teaching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_ethics#Politics).[^cite_note-114] In Europe, Christian democratic parties generally tend to be moderately conservative, with some being the main conservative party in their respective countries.[^cite_note-115] It originated as a reaction against the industrialization and urbanization associated with [*laissez-faire*-capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laissez-faire).[^cite_note-116] In post-war Europe, Christian-democratic parties dominated politics in several nations—the [Christian People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Democratic_and_Flemish) in Belgium, [CDU](60584.md) and [CSU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Social_Union_in_Bavaria) in Germany, [Fine Gael](11252.md) and [Fianna Fáil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fianna_F%C3%A1il) in Ireland, and [Christian Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Democracy_\(Italy\)) in Italy.[^cite_note-\:9-117] Many post-war Europeans saw Christian democracy as a moderate alternative to the extremes of right-wing nationalism and left-wing communism.[^cite_note-118] Christian-democratic parties were especially popular among European women, who often voted for these parties to a large extent due to their pro-family policies.[^cite_note-119]

### Social conservatism

Main article: [Social conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_conservatism)



A [March for Life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_for_Life_\(Paris\)) rally in Paris in 2012

Social conservatives believe that society is built upon a fragile network of relationships which need to be upheld through duty, traditional values, and established institutions; and that the government has a role in encouraging or enforcing traditional values or practices. A social conservative wants to preserve traditional morality and social [mores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mores), often by opposing what they consider radical policies or [social engineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_engineering_\(political_science\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeywood201769-120] Some social-conservative stances are the following:

- Support of a [culture of life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_life) and opposition to the destruction of human life at any stage, including [abortion](765.md), [embryonic stem cells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embryonic_stem_cell) research, and [euthanasia](9587.md).

- Support of [bioconservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bioconservatism) and opposition to both [eugenics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eugenics) and [transhumanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transhumanism).

  [^cite_note-121]

- Support of traditional [family values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_values), viewing the [nuclear family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_family) model as society's foundational unit.

- Support of a traditional definition of marriage as being one man and one woman, and opposition to expansion of [civil marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_marriage) and [child adoption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_adoption) to couples in [same-sex relationships](19771642.md).

- Support of [public morality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_morality) with prohibition of [drugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prohibition_\(drugs\)) and [prostitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prostitution) and censorship of [pornography](5710507.md).

- Support of [organized religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organized_religion) and opposition to [atheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheism) and [secularism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism), especially when militant.

  [^cite_note-MA1-122]

  [^cite_note-MA2-123]

  [^cite_note-124]

### Traditionalist conservatism

Main article: [Traditionalist conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditionalist_conservatism)

Traditionalist conservatism, also known as classical conservatism, emphasizes the need for the principles of [natural law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_law), transcendent moral order, [tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tradition), [hierarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hierarchy), [organicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organicism#In_politics_and_sociology), [agrarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrarianism), [classicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classicism), and [high culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_culture) as well as the intersecting spheres of [loyalty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loyalty).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrohnen2006870–875-125] Some traditionalists have embraced the labels [reactionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactionary) and [counter-revolutionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-revolution), defying the stigma that has attached to these terms since the [Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment). Having a hierarchical view of society, many traditionalist conservatives, including a few notable Americans such as [Ralph Adams Cram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ralph_Adams_Cram),[^cite_note-126] [William S. Lind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_S._Lind),[^cite_note-127] and [Charles A. Coulombe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_A._Coulombe),[^cite_note-128] defend the monarchical political structure as the most natural and beneficial social arrangement.

&#x20;\


## National variants

Conservative parties vary widely from country to country in the goals they wish to achieve.[^cite_note-\:6-4] Both conservative and [classical liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_liberalism) parties tend to favor private ownership of property, in opposition to [communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism), [socialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism), and [green](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_politics) parties, which favor communal ownership or laws regulating responsibility on the part of property owners. Where conservatives and liberals differ is primarily on social issues, where conservatives tend to reject behavior that does not conform to some [social norm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_norm). Modern conservative parties often define themselves by their opposition to liberal or socialist parties. The United States usage of the term conservative is unique to that country, where its first modern usage was for pro-free enterprise opponents of the [New Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Deal).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare199625–26-129]

Conservatism in Asia differs according to regions. [East Asia](19605700.md) and some [Southeast Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Asian) regions are deeply linked to [Confucian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confucian) traditions.

[Asian values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_values) is a [political ideology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_ideology) that attempts to define elements of society, culture and history common to the nations of Southeast and East Asia, particularly values of [commonality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sense_of_community) and [collectivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_organization) for social unity and economic good—contrasting with perceived [European ideals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_values) of the [universal rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_rights) of all individuals.

In [Western Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Asia), conservatism is often intertwined with [Islamism](15012.md), and religious law serves as the framework for social order and governance. This form of conservatism emphasizes the preservation of [Islamic law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_law) (*Sharia*) and traditional family structures against [secularism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism) and the encroachment of [Western cultural influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture). While some countries adopt a more traditional monarchy approach, others incorporate these conservative values into republican systems, but generally prioritize [religious identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_identity) and community morality as pillars of national stability.[^cite_note-130]

Chinese conservatism can be traced back to [Confucius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confucius), whose [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confucianism) is based on the values of loyalty, duty, and respect. He believed in a hierarchically organized society, [modeled after the patriarchal family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_as_a_model_for_the_state#Confucian_thought) and headed by an [absolute sovereign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_monarchy). However, Confucius also believed that the state should employ a [meritocratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meritocracy) class of administrators and advisers, recruited by [civil service exams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_examination). An alternative school of thought called [Legalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legalism_\(Chinese_philosophy\)) argued that administrative discipline, not Confucian virtue, was crucial for the governance of the state.[^cite_note-131]

For thousands of years, China was ruled by monarchs of various imperial dynasties. The [Mandate of Heaven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandate_of_Heaven) theory was invoked in order to legitimize the absolute authority of the Emperor.[^cite_note-132] The [Xinhai Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1911_Revolution) of 1911 overthrew [Puyi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puyi), the last Chinese Emperor, and ushered in the [Beiyang government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beiyang_government). Between 1927 and 1949, [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalist_government) was ruled by the nationalist party [Kuomintang](16903.md), which became right-wing after General [Chiang Kai-shek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiang_Kai-shek) purged communists from his party. Following his defeat in the [Chinese Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Civil_War) by the [Chinese Communist Party](7175.md) (CCP), Chiang continued ruling the island of [Taiwan](25734.md) until his death in 1975.[^cite_note-133]

On the mainland, Chinese conservatism was vehemently opposed and suppressed by the CCP, especially during the [Cultural Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Revolution). Members of the "[Five Black Categories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Black_Categories)"—landlords, rich farmers, counter-revolutionaries, bad influencers, and right-wingers—were violently persecuted. Young people formed cadres of [Red Guards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Guards) throughout the country and sought to destroy the [Four Olds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Olds): old ideas, old culture, old customs, and old habits—leading to the destruction of a large part of China's [cultural heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_heritage), including historical artifacts and religious sites.[^cite_note-134] Among them, some Red Guards who embraced local officials were pejoratively called "[conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Faction_\(Cultural_Revolution\))".[^cite_note-135]

In recent decades, Chinese conservatism has experienced a national revival.[^cite_note-136] The ancient schools of Confucianism and Legalism have made a return into mainstream Chinese thought.[^cite_note-137][^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139] [Wang Huning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wang_Huning), widely regarded as the [grey eminence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89minence_grise) and chief ideologue of the CCP, has criticized aspects of [Marxism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxism) and recommended that China combine its historical and modern values.[^cite_note-140] General Secretary [Xi Jinping](2017814.md) has called traditional [Chinese culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_culture) the "soul" of the nation and the "foundation" of the CCP.[^cite_note-141][^cite_note-142] China has also developed a form of [authoritarian capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian_capitalism#China) in recent years, further breaking with the orthodox communism of its past.[^cite_note-143] [Neoauthoritarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoauthoritarianism_\(China\)) is a current of political thought that advocates a powerful state to facilitate [market reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_and_opening_up).[^cite_note-144] A major concern of modern Chinese conservatism is the preservation of traditional culture.[^cite_note-145]

Indian politics has long been dominated by aristocratic and religious elites in one of the most hierarchically stratified nations in the world.[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147] In modern times, the [Bharatiya Janata Party](149330.md) (BJP), led by [Narendra Modi](444222.md), represents conservative politics. With over 170 million members as of October 2022, the BJP is by far the world's [largest political party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_political_parties).[^cite_note-148][^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150] It promotes [Hindu nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_nationalism), quasi-fascist [Hindutva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindutva), a hostile foreign policy against [Pakistan](23235.md), a hostile domestic policy against [Muslims in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslims_in_India) and a conservative social and fiscal policy.[^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154] The BJP movement is both elitist and populist, attracting privileged groups that fear encroachment on their dominant positions as well as "plebeian" groups that seek recognition around a majoritarian rhetoric of cultural pride, social order, and national strength.[^cite_note-155]

The [Pahlavi dynasty](338883.md) replaced the [Qajar dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar_dynasty) in 1925 after a *[coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1921_Persian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat)*, ruling Iran as a [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy) from 1925 until 1953 and then as an autocratic monarchy from the U.S.-instigated [1953 *coup d'état*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_Iranian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) until 1979.[^cite_note-156] In an attempt to introduce reform from above while preserving traditional relations of hierarchy, the [Shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah), [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](20065598.md), launched the [White Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Revolution) in 1963 as a series of reforms of aggressive modernization, resulting in a great [redistribution of wealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redistribution_of_wealth) from the aristocratic landlord class to Iran's working class and explosive economic growth in subsequent decades.[^cite_note-157] The [Iranian Revolution](347268.md) of 1979, supported by the clergy and the aristocracy, overthrew the monarchy and transformed the [Imperial State of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_State_of_Iran) to the [Islamic Republic of Iran](14653.md), thus replacing the [progressive conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_conservatism) of the Shah monarchy with the [reactionary conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactionary_conservatism) of Islamic theocracy.[^cite_note-158] The two main political camps in today's Iran are hard-line conservative[^cite_note-far-right-159] "[Principlists](46181393.md)" and Islamic liberal "[Reformists](491098.md)".[^cite_note-160]

After the declaration of the [State of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Israel), politics was initially dominated by left-wing parties, but overtime, right-wing parties became increasingly powerful, with conservatism now being the dominant ideology.[^cite_note-161][^cite_note-162] In the [2022 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Israeli_legislative_election), right-wing parties received 75 percent of the popular vote, a centrist party 17 percent, and left-wing parties 7 percent, and the subsequent government has been variously described as the most right-wing, as well as the most religious, in Israeli history.[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164]

Israeli conservatism is based around upholding [Jewish culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_culture), Jewish religious traditions, promotion of forms of [Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism) that tend to be more [irredentist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_irredentist_claims_or_disputes#Israel_and_Palestine) in nature (i. e. [Revisionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revisionist_Zionism) and [Neo-Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Zionism), which promote the idea of [Greater Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Israel), as compared to [Liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism#Liberal_Zionism) or [Labor Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labor_Zionism), which are supportive of a [two-state solution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-state_solution)), promoting Israeli [national security](240468.md), maintaining [the role of religion and the Rabbinate in the public sphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halachic_state), support for the [free market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_market), and [close ties with the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel-United_States_relations).[^cite_note-\:13-12]

Conservatism has been the dominant political ideology throughout modern Japanese history.[^cite_note-165][^cite_note-166] The right-wing conservative [Liberal Democratic Party](164231.md) has been the dominant ruling party since 1955, often referred to as the [1955 System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1955_System).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeywood201763–64-167] Therefore, some experts consider Japan a democratically elected [one-party state](102459.md) since the populace always votes for the same conservative party.[^cite_note-168]

Up until 1868, Japan was largely a [feudal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feudalism) state ruled by members of the aristocratic [Samurai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samurai) order with its *[bushido](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bushido)* code of honor. In the [Meiji era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meiji_era), a process of modernization, industrialization, and nationalization was initiated.[^cite_note-\:14-169] Power struggles between the old decentralized Samurai aristocracy and the new centralized imperial monarchy culminated in the [Satsuma Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satsuma_Rebellion) in 1877 with imperial victory.[^cite_note-170] During the era of [World War II](32927.md), Japan was transformed into an ultranationalist, imperialist state that conquered much of east and southeast Asia.[^cite_note-171] Contemporary conservatives, notably during the second premiership of [Shinzo Abe](2634434.md) from 2012 to 2020, advocate for revising the country's constitution, particularly [Article 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_9_of_the_Japanese_Constitution) which renounces war and prohibits Japan from maintaining a military.[^cite_note-172]

Japan is the oldest continuing monarchy in the history of mankind, with [Naruhito](266108.md) currently serving as [Emperor of Japan](10110.md).[^cite_note-173] In accordance with the principle of monarchy, Japanese society has an authoritarian family structure with a traditionalist fatherly authority that is primarily transferred to the oldest son.[^cite_note-174]

[Anti-communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communism#Japan_and_Manchukuo) and [anti-Chinese sentiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Chinese_sentiment_in_Japan) is widespread in Japan.[^cite_note-175] In 1925 the [Peace Preservation Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_Preservation_Law) was enacted with the aim of allowing the [Special Higher Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokubetsu_K%C5%8Dt%C5%8D_Keisatsu) to suppress socialists and communists more effectively.[^cite_note-176] In 1936 the [Empire of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Japan) and [Nazi Germany](21212.md) opposed the [Communist International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_International) by signing the [Anti-Comintern Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Comintern_Pact)—a pact later joined by the [Kingdom of Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Italy), [Francoist Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francoist_Spain), and the [Kingdom of Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hungary).[^cite_note-177] The Japanese term *[tenkō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenk%C5%8D)* refers to the coerced ideological conversions of Japanese socialists who were induced to renounce leftist ideology and enthusiastically embrace the monarchist, capitalist, and imperialist ideology favored by the state.[^cite_note-178] In the late 1940s and early 1950s, during the [Red Purge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Purge), tens of thousands of supporters of left-wing groups, especially those affiliated with the [Japanese Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_Communist_Party), were removed from their jobs in government, schools, and universities.[^cite_note-179]

*[Nippon Kaigi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nippon_Kaigi)* is an ultraconservative and ultranationalist organization that exerts a significant influence over contemporary Japanese politics. In 2014, a majority of [National Diet](163847.md) members were part of the group. Many ministers and a few prime ministers, including [Fumio Kishida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fumio_Kishida), [Tarō Asō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tar%C5%8D_As%C5%8D), [Shinzō Abe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shinz%C5%8D_Abe), and [Yoshihide Suga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoshihide_Suga), have been members.[^cite_note-180]

A highly developed and industrialized nation, Japan is more capitalistic and Western-oriented than other Asian nations. Therefore, some experts consider Japan part of the [Western world](21208200.md).[^cite_note-181] In 1960 [a treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Mutual_Cooperation_and_Security_between_the_United_States_and_Japan) was signed that established [a military alliance between the United States and Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S.%E2%80%93Japan_Alliance). However, the ultraconservative reactionary traditionalist [Yukio Mishima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yukio_Mishima) feared that his fellow Japanese were too enamored of modernization and Western-style capitalism to protect traditional [Japanese culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_culture).[^cite_note-182]

#### Singapore

Singapore's conservative party is the [People's Action Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Action_Party) (PAP), which promotes conservative values in the form of Asian democracy and [Asian values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_values).[^cite_note-183] These values include: nation before community and society above self; family as the basic unit of society; regard and community support for the individual; consensus instead of contention, and racial and religious harmony. They are a contrast against the "more Westernised, individualistic, and self-centred outlook on life" and uphold the "traditional Asian ideas of morality, duty and society".[^cite_note-SV-184]

The PAP is currently in [government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Singapore) and has been since independence in 1965. Having governed for over six decades, the PAP is the longest uninterrupted governing party among modern multiparty parliamentary democracies.[^cite_note-185] [Singapore](27318.md) is a [city state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_state) and has a reputation as a [nanny state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanny_state), owing to the considerable number of government [regulations and restrictions](1165902.md) on its citizens' lives.[^cite_note-186] Former [Prime Minister](350647.md) [Lee Kuan Yew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Kuan_Yew), the architect of the modern Singapore, observed: "If Singapore is a nanny state, then I am proud to have fostered one".[^cite_note-187] In an interview in the *[Straits Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Straits_Times)* in 1987, Lee said:

> I am often accused of interfering in the private lives of citizens. Yes, if I did not, had I not done that, we wouldn't be here today. And I say without the slightest remorse, that we wouldn't be here, we would not have made economic progress, if we had not intervened on very personal matters–who your neighbour is, how you live, the noise you make, how you spit, or what language you use. We decide what is right. Never mind what the people think.[^cite_note-188]

Conservatism has been the dominant political ideology throughout Korean history. [Syngman Rhee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syngman_Rhee) was a staunch anti-communist and became the first [President of South Korea](98630.md) upon the nation founding in 1948.[^cite_note-189] South Korean army general [Park Chung Hee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Park_Chung_Hee) seized power in the [May 16 coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_16_coup) of 1961, after which he was elected as the third President. He introduced the highly authoritarian [Yushin Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yushin_Constitution), ushering in the [Fourth Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Republic_of_Korea). He ruled the country as a dictator until [his assassination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Park_Chung_Hee) in 1979.[^cite_note-190]

Right-wing conservative parties have dominated South Korean politics for most of its modern history, while the main opposition parties have been moderate centrist and not left-wing. South Korea's major conservative party, the [People Power Party](63143730.md), has changed its form over the years. Its predecessor, the [Democratic-Liberal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Korea_Party) was led by [Roh Tae-woo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roh_Tae-woo), who was the first democratically elected president. The party was founded by the merging of [Roh Tae-woo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roh_Tae-woo)'s [Democratic Justice Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Justice_Party), [Kim Young Sam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Young_Sam)'s [Reunification Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reunification_Democratic_Party) and [Kim Jong-pil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Jong-pil)'s [New Democratic Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Democratic_Republican_Party). [Kim Young-sam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Young-sam) later became the seventh President of Korea.

When the conservative party was beaten by the opposition party in the general election of 1996, it became the [New Korea Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Korea_Party), but it changed again to the [Grand National Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty_Korea_Party) (GNP) a year later. Since the late [Kim Dae-jung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Dae-jung) assumed the presidency in 1998, GNP had been the opposition party until [Lee Myung-bak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Myung-bak) won the [presidential election of 2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_South_Korean_presidential_election).&#x20;

[Park Geun-hye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Park_Geun-hye) succeeded Lee in the [presidential election of 2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_South_Korean_presidential_election) till her impeachment in 2017. [Yoon Suk Yeol](62877952.md) won the [presidential election of 2022](63655548.md) by the narrowest margin since democratization till his impeachment in 2025.

European conservatism has taken many different expressions. Early forms were often [reactionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactionary) and [romantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism), idealizing the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages) and its [feudal social order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feudalism) with aristocratic rule and an established church.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEvon_Kuehnelt-Leddihn1943124-191][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENisbet200234–36-192] In the late 19th century, conservatism became increasingly [progressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_conservatism), adopting capitalism and espousing nationalism—which up until now had been anti-traditionalist and anti-imperialist forces.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeill202170–76-193] During the first half of the 20th century, as socialist movements were becoming more powerful and the [Tsarist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsar) regime was overthrown in the [Russian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Revolution), conservatism in Austria, Germany, Greece, Hungary, Italy, Portugal, Spain, and Romania transformed into the [far-right](221220.md), becoming more [authoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian_conservatism) and [extreme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultraconservatism).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlinkhorn19907-194] In the post-war era, conservatism assumed a more [moderate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moderate_conservatism) form with center-right Christian-democratic parties dominating politics across [Western Europe](33800.md) throughout the rest of the century,[^cite_note-\:9-117] although the authoritarian regimes of [Francoist Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francoist_Spain) and [Salazarian Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estado_Novo_\(Portugal\)) survived for a few more decades.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeywood201782-195] Towards the end of the century, after [the collapse of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union), conservatism took on a more [liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_conservatism) form. In recent decades, [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_conservatism) parties have been on the rise across Europe in opposition to [globalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalism).[^cite_note-196]

European countries, with the exception of [Switzerland](26748.md), have had a long monarchical tradition throughout history. Today, existing monarchies are [Andorra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Co-Princes_of_Andorra), [Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_Belgium), [Denmark](20714373.md), [Liechtenstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_Liechtenstein), [Luxembourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_Luxembourg), [Monaco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_Monaco), the [Netherlands](23416611.md), [Norway](4154892.md), [Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_Spain), [Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_Sweden), and the [United Kingdom](47063.md). Some reactionary movements in republican nations, such as *[Action Française](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Action_Fran%C3%A7aise)* in France, the [Monarchist National Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchist_National_Party) in Italy, and the [Black-Yellow Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black-Yellow_Alliance) in Austria, have advocated a restoration of the monarchy.

| [v](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:Conservatism_in_Austria)[t](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template_talk\:Conservatism_in_Austria)[e](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Template\:Conservatism_in_Austria) |

Austrian conservatism originated with Prince [Klemens von Metternich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klemens_von_Metternich), who was the architect behind the monarchist and imperialist [Conservative Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Order) that was enacted at the [Congress of Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Vienna) in the aftermath of the [French Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution) and the [Napoleonic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEncyclopædia_Britannica-24] The goal was to establish a [European balance of power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_balance_of_power) that could guarantee peace and suppress republican and nationalist movements.[^cite_note-197] During its existence, the [Austrian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrian_Empire) was the third most populous monarchy in Europe after the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) and the [United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland). Following its defeat in the [Austro-Prussian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austro-Prussian_War), it transformed into the [Austro-Hungarian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austro-Hungarian_Empire), which was the most diverse state in Europe with twelve nationalities living under a unifying monarch.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEvon_Kuehnelt-Leddihn1943139–140-198] The Empire was fragmented in the aftermath of [World War I](4764461.md), ushering in the democratic [First Austrian Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Austrian_Republic).

The [Austrian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrian_Civil_War) in 1934 saw a series of skirmishes between the right-wing government and socialist forces. When the insurgents were defeated, the government declared [martial law](50477.md) and held mass trials, forcing leading socialist politicians, such as [Otto Bauer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_Bauer), into exile.[^cite_note-199] The conservatives banned the [Social Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Democratic_Party_of_Austria) and replaced [parliamentary democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_democracy) with a [corporatist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_corporatism) and [clerical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clericalism) constitution. The [Fatherland Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatherland_Front_\(Austria\)), into which the paramilitary *[Heimwehr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heimwehr)* and the [Christian Social Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Social_Party_\(Austria\)) were merged, became the only legal political party in the resulting authoritarian regime, the [Federal State of Austria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_State_of_Austria).[^cite_note-200]

While having close ties to [Fascist Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascist_Italy_\(1922%E2%80%931943\)), which was still a [monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Italy) as well as a fellow Catholic nation, Austrian conservatives harbored strong anti-Prussian and anti-Nazi sentiment. Austria's most prominent conservative intellectual, the Catholic aristocrat [Erik von Kuehnelt-Leddihn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erik_von_Kuehnelt-Leddihn), published several books in which he interpreted [Nazism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism) as a [leftist](18499.md), [ochlocratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mob_rule), and [demagogic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demagogy) ideology opposed to the traditional rightist ideals of aristocracy, monarchy, and Christianity.[^cite_note-201] Austria's dictator [Engelbert Dollfuss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Engelbert_Dollfuss) saw Nazism as another form of totalitarian [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism), and he saw [Adolf Hitler](2731583.md) as the German version of [Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin). The conservatives banned the [Austrian Nazi Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrian_Nazi_Party) and arrested many of its activists, causing tens of thousands of Nazi sympathizers to flee to [Nazi Germany](21212.md) in order to avoid persecution.[^cite_note-202] A few months later, Nazi forces initiated the [July Putsch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_Putsch) and managed to assassinate Chancellor Dollfuss in an attempt to overthrow the conservative government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBischof2003-203] In response, [Benito Mussolini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benito_Mussolini) mobilized a part of the Italian army on the Austrian border and threatened Hitler with war in the event of a German invasion of Austria. In 1938, when Nazi Germany annexed Austria in the *[Anschluss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anschluss)*, conservative groups were suppressed: members of the [Austrian nobility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrian_nobility) and the [Catholic clergy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_clergy) were arrested and their properties were confiscated.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEvon_Kuehnelt-Leddihn1943210-204][^cite_note-205] [Otto von Hapsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_von_Hapsburg), the last [Crown Prince](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_heirs_to_the_Austrian_throne) of Austria-Hungary, was a fervent anti-Nazi, for which reason the Nazi regime ordered that he was to be executed immediately if caught.[^cite_note-206]

Following [World War II](32927.md) and the return to democracy, Austrian conservatives and socialists alike abandoned their extremism, believing in political compromise and seeking consensus in the middle.[^cite_note-207] The conservatives formed the [Austrian People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrian_People's_Party), which has been the major conservative party in Austria ever since. In contemporary politics, the party was led by [Sebastian Kurz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebastian_Kurz), whom the *[Frankfurter Allgemeine Zeitung](513356.md)* nicknamed the "young [Metternich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klemens_von_Metternich)".[^cite_note-208]

#### Belgium

Having its roots in the conservative [Catholic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Party_\(Belgium\)), the [Christian People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christen-Democratisch_en_Vlaams) retained a conservative edge through the 20th century, supporting the King in the [Royal Question](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Question), supporting nuclear family as the cornerstone of society, defending Christian education, and opposing [euthanasia](9587.md). The Christian People's Party dominated politics in post-war Belgium. In 1999, the party's support collapsed, and it became the country's fifth-largest party.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnnesley2005124-209][^cite_note-210][^cite_note-211] Since 2014, the [Flemish nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flemish_Movement) and conservative [New Flemish Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Flemish_Alliance) is the largest party in Belgium.[^cite_note-212]
