Danish conservatism emerged with the political grouping *[Højre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%B8jre)* (lit. 'Right'), which due to its alliance with King [Christian IX of Denmark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_IX_of_Denmark) dominated Danish politics and formed all governments from 1865 to 1901. When a constitutional reform in 1915 stripped the landed [gentry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentry) of political power, Højre was succeeded by the [Conservative People's Party of Denmark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_People's_Party_\(Denmark\)), which has since then been the main Danish conservative party.[^cite_note-Egander-213] Another Danish conservative party was the [Free Conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Conservatives), who were active between 1902 and 1920. Traditionally and historically, conservatism in Denmark has been more [populist](8354382.md#Denmark) and [agrarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrarianism) than in Sweden and Norway, where conservatism has been more [elitist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elitism) and [urban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanization).[^cite_note-214]

The Conservative People's Party led the government coalition from 1982 to 1993. The party had previously been member of various governments from 1916 to 1917, 1940 to 1945, 1950 to 1953, and 1968 to 1971. The party was a junior partner in governments led by the [Liberals](403109.md) from 2001 to 2011[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnnesley200568-215] and again from 2016 to 2019. The party is preceded by 11 years by the [Young Conservatives (KU)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Conservatives_\(Denmark\)), today the youth movement of the party.

The Conservative People's Party had a stable electoral support close to 15 to 20% at almost all general elections from 1918 to 1971. In the 1970s it declined to around 5%, but then under the leadership of [Poul Schlüter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poul_Schl%C3%BCter) reached its highest popularity level ever in [1984](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_Danish_general_election), receiving 23% of the votes. Since the late 1990s the party has obtained around 5 to 10% of the vote. In the [2026](72208286.md) Danish general election, the party received 7.6% of the vote.[^cite_note-216]

Conservative thinking has also influenced other Danish political parties. In 1995, the [Danish People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danish_People's_Party) was founded, based on a mixture of conservative, nationalist, and social-democratic ideas.[^cite_note-Egander-213] In 2007, the party [Liberal Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Alliance_\(Denmark\)) was established, combining liberal and conservative political ideas.[^cite_note-217] In 2022, the party [Danmarksdemokraterne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danmarksdemokraterne) was founded, describing itself as a party rooted in national, liberal, and conservative values.[^cite_note-218]

At the [2026 general election](72208286.md), parties commonly described as influenced by conservative ideas—including the Conservative People's Party, the Danish People's Party, Liberal Alliance, and Danmarksdemokraterne—together received approximately 31.9% of the vote.[^cite_note-219]

The conservative parties in Denmark have always considered the [monarchy](20714373.md) a central institution in Denmark.[^cite_note-220][^cite_note-221]

#### Finland

The conservative party in Finland is the [National Coalition Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Coalition_Party). The party was founded in 1918, when several monarchist parties united. Although right-wing in the past, today it is a moderate liberal-conservative party. While advocating economic liberalism, it is committed to the [social market economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_market_economy).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESiaroff2000243-222]

There has been strong [anti-Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Russian_sentiment) and [anti-communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communism) sentiment in Finland due to its long history of being invaded and conquered by Russia and the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-223][^cite_note-224] In the [Finnish Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Civil_War) of 1918, [White Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whites_\(Finland\)) defeated the leftist [Red Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Socialist_Workers'_Republic).[^cite_note-225] The [Finnish Defence Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_Defence_Forces) and the paramilitary [White Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Guard_\(Finland\)), led by Baron [Carl Gustaf Emil Mannerheim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Gustaf_Emil_Mannerheim), were assisted by the [German Imperial Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Army_\(German_Empire\)) at the request of the Finnish civil government. The far-right [Lapua movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapua_movement) continued to terrorize communists in post-war Finland, but it was banned after [a failed *coup d'état* attempt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A4nts%C3%A4l%C3%A4_rebellion) in 1932.[^cite_note-226]

Early conservatism in France focused on the rejection of the secularism of the French Revolution, support for the role of the Catholic Church, and the restoration of the monarchy.[^cite_note-227] After the first fall of [Napoleon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon) in 1814, the [House of Bourbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Bourbon) returned to power in the [Bourbon Restoration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourbon_Restoration_in_France). [Louis XVIII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XVIII) and [Charles X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_X_of_France), brothers of the executed King [Louis XVI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XVI), successively mounted the throne and instituted a conservative government intended to restore the proprieties, if not all the institutions, of the *[Ancien Régime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancien_R%C3%A9gime)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFawcett202020–21-228]

After the [July Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_Revolution) of 1830, [Louis Philippe I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Philippe_I), a member of the more liberal [Orléans branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Orl%C3%A9ans) of the House of Bourbon, proclaimed himself as King of the French. The [Second French Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_French_Empire) saw an Imperial [Bonapartist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonapartist) regime of [Napoleon III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon_III) from 1852 to 1870.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeywood201775-229] The Bourbon monarchist cause was on the verge of victory in the 1870s, but then collapsed because the proposed king, [Henri, Count of Chambord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri,_Count_of_Chambord), refused to fly the tri-colored flag.[^cite_note-230] The turn of the century saw the rise of *[Action Française](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Action_Fran%C3%A7aise)*—an ultraconservative, reactionary, nationalist, and royalist movement that advocated a restoration of the monarchy.[^cite_note-231]

Tensions between Christian rightists and secular leftists heightened in the 1890–1910 era, but moderated after the spirit of unity in fighting World War I.[^cite_note-232] An authoritarian form of conservatism characterized the [Vichy regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vichy_France) of 1940–1944 under Marshal [Philippe Pétain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippe_P%C3%A9tain) with heightened antisemitism, opposition to individualism, emphasis on family life, and national direction of the economy.[^cite_note-\:10-59]

Conservatism has been the major political force in France since World War II,[^cite_note-233] although the number of conservative groups and their lack of stability defy simple categorization.[^cite_note-\:14-169] Following the war, conservatives supported [Gaullist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaullism) groups and parties, espoused [nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_nationalism), and emphasized tradition, social order, and the regeneration of France.[^cite_note-234] Unusually, post-war conservatism in France was formed around the personality of a leader—army general and aristocrat [Charles de Gaulle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle) who led the [Free French Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_France) against Nazi Germany—and it did not draw on traditional French conservatism, but on the [Bonapartist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonapartism) tradition.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWare199632-235] Gaullism in France continues under [The Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Republicans_\(France\)) (formerly [Union for a Popular Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_for_a_Popular_Movement)), a party previously led by [Nicolas Sarkozy](557007.md), who served as President of France from 2007 to 2012 and whose ideology is known as [Sarkozysm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarkozysm).[^cite_note-236]

In 2021, the French intellectual [Éric Zemmour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89ric_Zemmour) founded the nationalist party [Reconquête](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconqu%C3%AAte), which has been described as a more rightist version of [Marine Le Pen](572897.md)'s [National Rally](67119.md).[^cite_note-237]

| [v](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:Conservatism_in_Germany)[t](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template_talk\:Conservatism_in_Germany)[e](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Template\:Conservatism_in_Germany) |

Germany was the heart of the reactionary [Romantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism) movement that swept Europe in the aftermath of the progressive [Age of Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment) and its culmination in the anti-conservative French Revolution.[^cite_note-\:3-106] [German Romanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Romanticism) was deeply [organicist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organicism#In_politics_and_sociology) and [medievalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medievalism), finding expression philosophically among the [Old Hegelians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_Hegelians) and judicially in the [German historical school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_historical_school).[^cite_note-238] Prominent conservative exponents were [Friedrich Schlegel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Schlegel), [Novalis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novalis), [Wilhelm Heinrich Wackenroder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilhelm_Heinrich_Wackenroder), [Friedrich Carl von Savigny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Carl_von_Savigny), and [Adam Müller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_M%C3%BCller).[^cite_note-239]

&#x20;During the second half of the 19th century, German conservatism developed alongside [nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_nationalism), culminating in Germany's victory over France in the [Franco-Prussian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco-Prussian_War), the creation of the unified [German Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Empire) in 1871, and the simultaneous rise of ”Iron Chancellor” [Otto von Bismarck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_von_Bismarck) on the European political stage. Bismarck's [balance of power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balance_of_power_\(international_relations\)) model maintained peace in Europe for decades at the end of the 19th century.[^cite_note-240] His "revolutionary conservatism" was a conservative state-building strategy, based on [class collaboration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_collaboration) and designed to make ordinary Germans—not just the [Junker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junker_\(Prussia\)) aristocracy—more loyal to state and [Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Emperor).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEncyclopædia_Britannica-24] He created the modern [welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state) in Germany in the 1880s.[^cite_note-241] According to scholars, his strategy was:&#x20;

> granting social rights to enhance the integration of a hierarchical society, to forge a bond between workers and the state so as to strengthen the latter, to maintain traditional relations of authority between social and status groups, and to provide a countervailing power against the modernist forces of liberalism and socialism.[^cite_note-242]

Bismarck also enacted [universal manhood suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_manhood_suffrage) in the new German Empire in 1871.[^cite_note-243] He became a great hero to German conservatives, who erected many monuments to his memory after he left office in 1890.[^cite_note-244]

During the [interwar period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interwar_period)—after Germany's defeat in World War I, the abdication of Emperor [Wilhelm II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilhelm_II), and the introduction of parliamentary democracy—German conservatives experienced a cultural crisis and felt uprooted by a progressively modernist world.[^cite_note-245] This angst was expressed philosophically in the [Conservative Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Revolution) movement with prominent exponents such as historian [Oswald Spengler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oswald_Spengler), jurist [Carl Schmitt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Schmitt), and author [Ernst Jünger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst_J%C3%BCnger).[^cite_note-246] The major conservative party of this era was the reactionary [German National People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_National_People's_Party), who advocated a restored monarchy.[^cite_note-247]

With the rise of [Nazism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism) in 1933, [traditional agrarian movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrarian_conservatism_in_Germany) faded and were supplanted by a more command-based economy and forced social integration. [Adolf Hitler](2731583.md) succeeded in garnering the support of many German industrialists; but prominent traditionalists, including military officers [Claus von Stauffenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claus_von_Stauffenberg) and [Henning von Tresckow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henning_von_Tresckow), pastor [Dietrich Bonhoeffer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dietrich_Bonhoeffer), Bishop [Clemens August Graf von Galen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clemens_August_Graf_von_Galen), and monarchist [Carl Friedrich Goerdeler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Friedrich_Goerdeler), openly and secretly opposed his policies of euthanasia, genocide, and attacks on organized religion.[^cite_note-248] The former German Emperor [Wilhelm II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilhelm_II) was highly critical of Hitler, writing in 1938:

> There's a man alone, without family, without children, without God ... He builds legions, but he doesn't build a nation. A nation is created by families, a religion, traditions: it is made up out of the hearts of mothers, the wisdom of fathers, the joy and the exuberance of children ... This man could bring home victories to our people each year, without bringing them either glory or danger. But of our Germany, which was a nation of poets and musicians, of artists and soldiers, he has made a nation of hysterics and hermits, engulfed in a mob and led by a thousand liars or fanatics.[^cite_note-\:7-71]

Post-[World War II](32927.md) Germany developed a special form of conservatism called [ordoliberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordoliberalism), which is centered around the concept of [ordered liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordered_liberty).[^cite_note-249] Neither socialist nor capitalist, it promotes a compromise between state and market, and argues that the national culture of a country must be taken into account when implementing economic policies.[^cite_note-250] [Alexander Rüstow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_R%C3%BCstow) and [Wilhelm Röpke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilhelm_R%C3%B6pke) were two prominent exponents of this economic theory, and its implementation is largely credited as a reason behind the [German miracle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wirtschaftswunder)—the rapid reconstruction and development of the war-wrecked economies of [West Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Germany) and [Austria](26964606.md) after World War II.[^cite_note-251]

More recently, the work of conservative [Christian Democratic Union](60584.md) leader and Chancellor [Helmut Kohl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helmut_Kohl) helped bring about [German reunification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_reunification), along with the closer [European integration](683704.md) in the form of the [Maastricht Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maastricht_Treaty). Today, German conservatism is often associated with politicians such as Chancellor [Angela Merkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angela_Merkel), whose tenure was marked by attempts to save the common European currency ([Euro](9472.md)) from demise. The German conservatives were divided under Merkel due to the refugee crisis in Germany, and many conservatives in the [CDU/CSU](10796483.md) opposed the immigration policies developed under Merkel.[^cite_note-252] The 2020s also saw the rise of the right-wing populist [Alternative for Germany](38708229.md).[^cite_note-253]

The main inter-war conservative party was called the [People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Party_\(Greece\)) (PP), which supported [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_monarchy) and opposed the [republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Hellenic_Republic) [Liberal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Party_\(Greece\)). Both parties were suppressed by the authoritarian, arch-conservative, and royalist [4th of August Regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/4th_of_August_Regime) of General [Ioannis Metaxas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ioannis_Metaxas) in 1936–1941. The PP was able to re-group after World War II as part of a United Nationalist Front which achieved power campaigning on a simple anti-communist, nationalist platform during the [Greek Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Civil_War) in 1946–1949. However, the vote received by the PP declined during the so-called "Centrist Interlude" in 1950–1952.

In 1952, Marshal [Alexandros Papagos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandros_Papagos) created the [Greek Rally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Rally) as an umbrella for the right-wing forces. The Greek Rally came to power in 1952 and remained the leading party in Greece until 1963. After Papagos' death in 1955, it was reformed as the [National Radical Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Radical_Union) under [Konstantinos Karamanlis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konstantinos_Karamanlis). Right-wing governments backed by the palace and the army overthrew the [Centre Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_Union) government in 1965 and governed the country until the establishment of the far-right [Greek junta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_junta) (1967–1974). After the [regime's collapse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metapolitefsi) in August 1974, Karamanlis returned from exile to lead the government and founded the [New Democracy](424225.md) party. The new conservative party had four objectives: to confront [Turkish expansionism in Cyprus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_invasion_of_Cyprus), to reestablish and solidify democratic rule, to give the country a strong government, and to make a powerful moderate party a force in Greek politics.[^cite_note-254]

The [Independent Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_Greeks), a newly formed political party in [Greece](12108.md), has also supported conservatism, particularly [national](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_conservatism) and [religious conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_conservatism). The Founding Declaration of the Independent Greeks strongly emphasizes the preservation of the Greek state and its sovereignty, the [Greek people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_people), and the [Greek Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church).

Contemporary conservatism and [liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism_and_radicalism_in_Hungary) in Hungary have their origins in the long debates during the [Hungarian Reform Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_Reform_Era), primarily between the conservative but reformist [István Széchenyi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istv%C3%A1n_Sz%C3%A9chenyi) and the liberal and revolutionary [Lajos Kossuth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lajos_Kossuth). Széchenyi favored policies such as concern for the [aristocracy in Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_nobility), preserving Hungary's autonomy within the [Habsburg Realm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrian_Empire), limited suffrage for wealthy and educated men, promoting a more [isolationist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isolationist) foreign policy, and supporting [British-style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Saxon_model) [laissez-faire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laissez-faire) economics.[^cite_note-sasvari-255][^cite_note-zavodszky-256][^cite_note-257] All of this contrasts Kossuth's demands for a [republican form of government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republicanism), full Hungarian independence, [universal suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_suffrage), a [liberal-internationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_internationalism) foreign policy, and protectionist economics to develop industry.[^cite_note-sasvari-255] The [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(Hungary\)) was founded in 1846, initially as a rival to the [Opposition Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opposition_Party_\(Hungary\)) of Kossuth and [Lajos Batthyány](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lajos_Batthy%C3%A1ny), founded in 1847, but became allies during the [Hungarian Revolution of 1848](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_Revolution_of_1848) and eventually merged into the Opposition Party after [Hungarian independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_Declaration_of_Independence) and the founding of the brief [Hungarian State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_State) in 1849.[^cite_note-zavodszky-256]

The dominance of the political right of [inter-war Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungary_between_the_two_world_wars), after the collapse of a short-lived communist regime, was described by historian [István Deák](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istv%C3%A1n_De%C3%A1k):

> Between 1919 and 1944 Hungary was a rightist country. Forged out of a counter-revolutionary heritage, its governments advocated a "[nationalist Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_nationalism)" policy; they extolled heroism, faith, and unity; they despised the French Revolution, and they spurned the liberal and socialist ideologies of the 19th century. The governments saw Hungary as a bulwark against [bolshevism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolshevism) and bolshevism's instruments: [socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism), [cosmopolitanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmopolitanism), and [Freemasonry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freemasonry). They perpetrated the rule of a small clique of aristocrats, civil servants, and army officers, and surrounded with adulation the head of the state, the counterrevolutionary [Admiral Horthy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikl%C3%B3s_Horthy).[^cite_note-258]

Horthy's authoritarian conservative regime suppressed communists and fascists alike, banning the [Hungarian Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_Communist_Party) as well as the fascist [Arrow Cross Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrow_Cross_Party). The fascist leader [Ferenc Szálasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferenc_Sz%C3%A1lasi) was repeatedly imprisoned at Horthy's command.[^cite_note-\:8-60]

Since 2010, [Viktor Orbán](750582.md) of the [Fidesz](404647.md) party has been [Prime Minister of Hungary](378359.md). Orbán's positions are a blend of [soft Euroscepticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft_Euroscepticism), [right-wing populism](8354382.md), and [national conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_conservatism).[^cite_note-259][^cite_note-260][^cite_note-261] [Zsolt Enyedi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zsolt_Enyedi?action=edit\&redlink=1) has argued that illiberal conservatism plays a major role in Fidesz's ideology.[^cite_note-262] Multiple expressions of conservative ideology has also been found within the 2011 [Constitution of Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Hungary).[^cite_note-263]

[CPAC Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CPAC_Hungary) is an annual political conference, The first CPAC Hungary took place in 2022.

Founded in 1924 as the [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(Iceland\)), Iceland's [Independence Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Party_\(Iceland\)) adopted its current name in 1929 after the merger with the [Liberal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Party_\(Iceland,_historical\)). From the beginning, they have been the largest vote-winning party, averaging around 40%. They combined liberalism and conservatism, supported nationalization of infrastructure, and advocated [class collaboration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_collaboration). While mostly in opposition during the 1930s, they embraced economic liberalism, but accepted the welfare state after the war and participated in governments supportive of state intervention and protectionism. Unlike other Scandanivian conservative (and liberal) parties, it has always had a large working-class following.[^cite_note-264] After the financial crisis in 2008, the support level has dropped to 20–25%.

#### Ireland

Conservatism in Ireland historically revolved around social policies relating to the [Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Ireland) as well as a commitment to [Irish republicanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_republicanism), [Irish neutrality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_neutrality), [anti-abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-abortion_movements#Ireland), [anti-communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communism), [pro-Europeanism](631596.md), and, more recently, [anti-immigration](6491626.md#Ireland).

During the presidency of [Éamon de Valera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89amon_de_Valera), a broad array of [Catholic social policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89amon_de_Valera#Catholic_social_policy) were enacted, mostly with the goals of winning devout, rural, conservative voters, most of whom welcomed these policies.[^cite_note-265][^cite_note-266] Such policies included writing into the [Constitution of Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Ireland) that a woman's place was in the home, prohibiting the [importation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Importation) or [sale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sales) of [contraceptives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contraceptives), and enactment of strict censorship laws.[^cite_note-267]

[Fianna Fáil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fianna_F%C3%A1il) and its historic rival, [Fine Gael](11252.md), are both considered historically to be conservative parties. However, there are some differences: mainly, Fianna Fáil is usually considered more republican,[^cite_note-268][^cite_note-269] while Fine Gael tends to be more classically liberal.[^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271]

Starting in 2022, a [series of protests calling for a reduction in illegal immigration](72416997.md) have become more commonplace in Ireland, mostly over the status of [temporary asylum seeker shelters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transitional_shelter) were unable to accommodate the more than 65,000 refugees.[^cite_note-272]

After the [unification of Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unification_of_Italy), the country was governed successively by the [Historical Right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_Right), which represented conservative, liberal-conservative, and conservative-liberal positions, and the [Historical Left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_Left). After [World War I](4764461.md), the country saw the emergence of its first mass parties, notably including the [Italian People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_People's_Party_\(1919\)) (PPI), a Christian-democratic party that sought to represent the [Catholic](606848.md) majority, which had long refrained from politics. The PPI and the [Italian Socialist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Socialist_Party) decisively contributed to the loss of strength and authority of the old liberal ruling class, which had not been able to structure itself into a proper party: the [Liberal Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Union_\(Italy\)) was not coherent and the [Italian Liberal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Liberal_Party) came too late.

In 1921, [Benito Mussolini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benito_Mussolini) founded the [National Fascist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Fascist_Party) (PNF), and the next year, through the [March on Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_on_Rome), he was appointed [Prime Minister](273741.md) by King [Victor Emmanuel III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Emmanuel_III). Fascism originated as a populist, revolutionary, anti-royalist, anti-clerical, anti-capitalist, and anti-conservative ideology,[^cite_note-273] viewed by many socialists as a leftist heresy rather than a rightist opponent, but it transformed and became distinctly right-wing when it made compromises with the conservative establishment in order to consolidate authority and suppress communist movements.[^cite_note-274][^cite_note-275] Mussolini commented on the dynamic pragmatism of fascism:

> We do not believe in dogmatic programs. ... We permit ourselves the luxury of being aristocratic and democratic, conservative and progressive, reactionary and revolutionary, legalists and illegalists, according to the circumstances of the moment, the place and the environment.[^cite_note-276]

In 1926, all parties were dissolved except the PNF, which remained the only legal party in the [Kingdom of Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Italy) until the fall of the regime in July 1943. By 1945, fascists were discredited, disbanded, and outlawed, while Mussolini was executed in April that year.[^cite_note-277] The [1946 Italian institutional referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1946_Italian_institutional_referendum) concerned the fate of the monarchy. While southern Italy and parts of northern Italy were royalist, other parts, especially in central Italy, were predominantly republican. The outcome was 54–46% in favor of a republic, leading to a collapse of the monarchy.[^cite_note-278]

After [World War II](32927.md), the center-right was dominated by the centrist party [Christian Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Democracy_\(Italy\)) (DC), which included both conservative and center-left elements.[^cite_note-279] With its landslide victory over the [Italian Socialist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Socialist_Party) and the [Italian Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Communist_Party) in 1948, the political center was in power. In [Denis Mack Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denis_Mack_Smith)'s words, it was "moderately conservative, reasonably tolerant of everything which did not touch religion or property, but above all Catholic and sometimes [clerical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clericalism)".[^cite_note-280] DC dominated politics until its dissolution in 1994, having governed for 47 out of 52 years.[^cite_note-\:9-117]

In 1994, entrepreneur and media tycoon [Silvio Berlusconi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silvio_Berlusconi) founded the liberal-conservative party [Forza Italia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forza_Italia_\(1994\)) (FI). He won three elections in [1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Italian_general_election), [2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_Italian_general_election), and [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Italian_general_election), governing the country for almost ten years as prime minister. FI formed a coalitions with several parties, including the national-conservative [National Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Alliance_\(Italy\)) (AN), heir of the MSI, and the regionalist [Lega Nord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lega_Nord) (LN). FI was briefly incorporated, along with AN, in [The People of Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_People_of_Freedom) party and later revived in the new [Forza Italia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forza_Italia_\(2013\)).[^cite_note-281] After the [2018 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Italian_general_election), the LN and the [Five Star Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Star_Movement) formed a populist government, which lasted about a year.[^cite_note-282] In the [2022 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Italian_general_election), a [center-right coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre-right_coalition_\(Italy\)) came to power, this time dominated by [Brothers of Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brothers_of_Italy) (FdI), a new national-conservative party born on the ashes of AN. Consequently, FdI, the re-branded [Lega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lega_\(political_party\)), and FI formed a government under FdI leader [Giorgia Meloni](29228976.md).

#### Luxembourg

Luxembourg's major conservative party, the [Christian Social People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Social_People's_Party), was formed as the Party of the Right in 1914 and adopted its present name in 1945. It was consistently the largest political party in Luxembourg and dominated politics throughout the 20th century.[^cite_note-283]

#### Netherlands

Liberalism has been strong in the Netherlands. Therefore, rightist parties are often liberal-conservative or conservative-liberal. One example is the [People's Party for Freedom and Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Party_for_Freedom_and_Democracy). Even the right-wing populist and far-right [Party for Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_for_Freedom), which dominated the [2023 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Dutch_general_election), supports liberal positions such as gay rights, abortion, and euthanasia.[^cite_note-284]

#### Norway

The [Conservative Party of Norway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(Norway\)) (Norwegian: *Høyre*, literally "Right") was formed by the old upper-class of state officials and wealthy merchants to fight the populist democracy of the [Liberal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Party_\(Norway\)), but it lost power in 1884, when parliamentarian government was first practiced. It formed its first government under parliamentarism in 1889 and continued to alternate in power with the Liberals until the 1930s, when [Labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_Party_\(Norway\)) became the dominant party. It has elements both of [paternalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paternalism), stressing the responsibilities of the state, and of [economic liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalism). It first returned to power in the 1960s.[^cite_note-285] During [Kåre Willoch's premiership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%A5re_Willoch#Premiership) in the 1980s, much emphasis was laid on liberalizing the credit and housing market and abolishing the [NRK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NRK) TV and radio monopoly, while supporting [law and order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_and_order_\(politics\)) in criminal justice and traditional norms in education.[^cite_note-286]

The dominant conservative party in Poland is [Law and Justice](520309.md) (PiS). Polish conservatism is characterized by social and cultural conservatism, patriotism, adherence to [Catholic social teaching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_social_teaching), and cooperation with the [Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Poland).[^cite_note-Dudek-287] Contemporary Polish conservatives believe in [Atlanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanticism) and strong relations with the United States, meanwhile taking a stand against Russia.[^cite_note-Kaczyński-288]

PiS has taken a populist and statist approach to economics, expanding state control over industries and media, greatly expanding social welfare and applying [Keynesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keynesian)-esque "anti-crisis shields",[^cite_note-Dudek-287] differentiating itself from previous conservative political parties and movements like [AWS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solidarity_Electoral_Action) or [Endecja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democracy_\(Poland\))[^cite_note-Mackiewicz-289] which believed in economic liberalism. The party would "regulate the market strongly via the active role of the state",[^cite_note-folvarcny_2020-290] pursuing greater regulations against banks, large enterprises and the stock market;[^cite_note-folvarcny_2020-290] at the same time, it also attempted to implement deregulation for small businesses and enterprises.[^cite_note-291] Another difference to AWS is PiS' euroscepticism.[^cite_note-Dudek-287] Though not opposing European Union membership, PiS pursues an assertive policy of conflict with the [European Commission](9974.md),[^cite_note-Kaczyński-288] which, in reaction, took a hostile stance against PiS. In the European Parliament, PiS belongs to the [European Conservatives and Reformists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Conservatives_and_Reformists_Group) group. Liberal media in Poland is vehemently biased against PiS and opposed to its rule, often calling it authoritarian.[^cite_note-Dudek-287] Liberal scholar Antoni Dudek rejects giving PiS the authoritarian label, suggesting that PiS rejects the ideals of [liberal democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_democracy) and instead embraces a "national democratic" or [illiberal democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illiberal_democracy) form of governance.[^cite_note-Dudek-287]

In the preceding [interwar period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interwar_period), Poland's conservative movement was split between the "Old" Galician and Kresy conservatives, usually landowners, which formed minor parties like the [State Unity in the Kresy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Unity_in_the_Kresy), and the "New Conservative" movement of [National Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democracy_\(Poland\)) (Endecja) under [Roman Dmowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Dmowski), which was oriented around the urban [intelligentsia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelligentsia) and [petite bourgeoise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petite_bourgeoise). The latter sometimes cooperated with the right-wing factions of the [Polish peasant movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peasant_movement#Poland), affiliated under the [PSL "Piast"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_People's_Party_%22Piast%22_\(1913%E2%80%931931\)) that cooperated with Endecja, creating a [common government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chjeno-Piast) under the [Lanckorona Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lanckorona_Pact), although the peasant movement was still not a part of the conservative movement. Endecja espoused [Russophilia](2541825.md) and believed in cooperation with the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) and later the [White Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Army). They also advocated maintenance of democracy, [civic nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civic_nationalism), and [parliamentarism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentarism), opposing [Józef Piłsudski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B3zef_Pi%C5%82sudski)'s [BBWR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonpartisan_Bloc_for_Cooperation_with_the_Government)'s attempts at [empowering the presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_republic) and later its absolute seizure of power.[^cite_note-Mackiewicz-289] In contrast, many Old Conservatives, such as [Stanisław Cat-Mackiewicz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanis%C5%82aw_Cat-Mackiewicz), found themselves as allies of Marshal Piłsudski.[^cite_note-Mackiewicz-289] Endecja rejected [Romanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism_in_Poland) and [Messianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_of_Europe), concepts which were important to the Old Conservatives.[^cite_note-rd-292]

| [v](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:Conservatism_in_Russia)[t](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template_talk\:Conservatism_in_Russia)[e](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Template\:Conservatism_in_Russia) |

Russian conservatism has experienced a revival in recent decades.[^cite_note-293] Under [Vladimir Putin](32817.md), the dominant leader since 1999, Russia has promoted explicitly conservative policies in social, cultural, and political matters, both at home and abroad.[^cite_note-294] Putin has criticized globalism and economic liberalism, claiming that "liberalism has become obsolete" and that the vast majority of people in the world oppose [multiculturalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiculturalism), [free immigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_immigration), and [rights for LGBT people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_by_country_or_territory).[^cite_note-295] Russian conservatism is special in some respects as it supports a [mixed economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_economy) with [economic intervention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_interventionism), combined with a strong nationalist sentiment and [social conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_conservatism) which is largely [populist](8354382.md). As a result, Russian conservatism opposes [right-libertarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right-libertarianism) ideals such as the aforementioned concept of economic liberalism found in other conservative movements around the world.

Putin has also promoted new [think tanks](37101.md) that bring together like-minded intellectuals and writers. For example, the [Izborsky Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Izborsky_Club), founded in 2012 by [Alexander Prokhanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Prokhanov), stresses [Russian nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_nationalism), the restoration of Russia's historical greatness, and systematic opposition to liberal ideas and policies.[^cite_note-296] [Vladislav Surkov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladislav_Surkov), a senior government official, has been one of the key ideologues during Putin's presidency.[^cite_note-297]

In cultural and social affairs, Putin has collaborated closely with the [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church).[^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299] Under [Patriarch Kirill of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarch_Kirill_of_Moscow), the Church has backed the expansion of Russian power into Crimea and eastern Ukraine.[^cite_note-300] More broadly, *[The New York Times](30680.md)* reports in September 2016 how the Church's policy prescriptions support the Kremlin's appeal to social conservatives:

> A fervent foe of homosexuality and any attempt to put individual rights above those of family, community, or nation, the Russian Orthodox Church helps project Russia as the natural ally of all those who pine for a more secure, illiberal world free from the tradition-crushing rush of globalization, multiculturalism, and women's and gay rights.[^cite_note-301]

Conservatism in Spain is represented by the center-right [People's Party](404727.md) and ultra-right [Vox party](41928488.md). The People's Party has its roots in the People's Alliance founded on 9 October 1976 by former [Francoist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francoist_Spain) minister [Manuel Fraga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Fraga). Although Fraga was a member of the reformist faction of the Franco regime, he supported an extremely gradual transition to democracy. However, he badly underestimated the public's distaste for Francoism. Additionally, while he attempted to convey a reformist image, the large number of former Francoists in the party led the public to perceive it as both [reactionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactionary) and [authoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism). In the June [1977 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1977_Spanish_general_election), the AP garnered only 8.3 percent of the vote, putting it in fourth place.

The PP joined the [European People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_People's_Party) in 1991.[^cite_note-JansenHecke2011-302]

The PP became the largest party for the first time in 1996, and [José María Aznar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Mar%C3%ADa_Aznar) became Prime Minister with the support of the [Basque Nationalist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basque_Nationalist_Party), the [Catalan](6822.md) [Convergence and Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convergence_and_Union) and the [Canarian Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canarian_Coalition). In the 2000 elections, the PP gained an [absolute majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_majority).

In August 2003, [Mariano Rajoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariano_Rajoy) was appointed Secretary General by Aznar. Thus, Rajoy became the party's candidate for Prime Minister in the [2004 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Spanish_general_election), held three days after the [11 March 2004 Madrid train bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/11_March_2004_Madrid_train_bombings), and which Rajoy lost by a big margin to [Spanish Socialist Workers' Party](272596.md) (PSOE) leader [José Luis Rodríguez Zapatero](19614411.md).

The PP under [Mariano Rajoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariano_Rajoy) opposed the PSOE government after the PP lost the [general election in 2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Spanish_general_election), arguing that this victory was influenced by the [Madrid bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Madrid_train_bombings) of 11 March 2004. At a national level, its political strategy has followed two main axes, both linked to Spain's delicate regional politics: first, opposing further administrative devolution to Catalonia by means of the newly approved "Estatut" or Statute of Catalonia that lays out the powers of the Catalan regional government. Second, it remains opposed to political negotiations with the Basque separatist organization [ETA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ETA_\(separatist_group\)).

The prospect of increased demands for autonomy in the programs of Catalan and Basque parties, and Zapatero's alleged favoring of them, became a focus for the party's campaign for the [March 2008 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Spanish_general_election). [Basque President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lehendakari) [Juan José Ibarretxe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Jos%C3%A9_Ibarretxe)'s proposal for a unilateral referendum for the solution of the [Basque Conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basque_Conflict) was another important issue.

The People's Party under Rajoy has an increasingly patriotic, or [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalism), element to it, appealing to the sense of "Spanishness" and making strong use of national symbols such as the [Spanish flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Spain). Prior to the national celebrations of Spanish Heritage Day, Rajoy made a speech asking Spaniards to "privately or publicly" display their pride in their nation and to honor their flag, an action which received some criticism from many political groups of the Congress.

Vox was founded in December 2013, splitting from the [People's Party](404727.md) (PP).[^cite_note-Reuters1-303] The [party platform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_platform) called for the rewriting of the [Spanish constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_constitution) so as to curb regional autonomy and abolish regional parliaments.[^cite_note-Reuters1-303] Several founding members of the party (for example, [Alejo Vidal-Quadras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejo_Vidal-Quadras_Roca), [José Antonio Ortega Lara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Antonio_Ortega_Lara), and [Santiago Abascal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santiago_Abascal)) had been members of the platform "reconversion.es", which had issued a manifesto in 2012 calling for a [recentralization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_unitarism) of the State.[^cite_note-casals2019pp-304] Vidal-Quadras was proclaimed as the first chairman in March 2014.[^cite_note-305]

In the early 19th century, Swedish conservatism developed alongside [Swedish Romanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish_Romantic_literature). The historian [Erik Gustaf Geijer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erik_Gustaf_Geijer), an exponent of [Gothicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothicism), glorified the [Viking Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viking_Age) and the [Swedish Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish_Empire),[^cite_note-306] and the idealist philosopher [Christopher Jacob Boström](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Jacob_Bostr%C3%B6m) became the chief ideologue of the official state doctrine, which dominated Swedish politics for almost a century.[^cite_note-307] Other influential Swedish conservative Romantics were [Esaias Tegnér](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esaias_Tegn%C3%A9r) and [Per Daniel Amadeus Atterbom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Per_Daniel_Amadeus_Atterbom).

Early parliamentary conservatism in Sweden was explicitly elitist. The [Conservative Party](202985.md) was formed in 1904 with one major goal in mind: to stop the advent of [universal suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_suffrage), which they feared would result in socialism. Yet, it was a Swedish admiral, the conservative politician [Arvid Lindman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arvid_Lindman), who first extended democracy by enacting [male suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Male_suffrage), despite the protests of more traditionalist voices, such as the later prime minister, the arch-conservative and authoritarian statesman [Ernst Trygger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst_Trygger), who railed at progressive policies such as the abolition of the [death penalty](5902.md).[^cite_note-308]

Once a democratic system was in place, Swedish conservatives sought to combine traditional elitism with modern populism. Sweden's most renowned political scientist, the conservative politician [Rudolf Kjellén](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudolf_Kjell%C3%A9n), coined the terms [geopolitics](163225.md) and [biopolitics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biopolitics) in relation to his [organic theory of the state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organicism#In_politics_and_sociology).[^cite_note-309] He also developed the [corporatist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_corporatism)-nationalist concept of *[Folkhemmet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folkhemmet)* ('the people's home'), which became the single most powerful political concept in Sweden throughout the 20th century, although it was adopted by the [Social Democratic Party](202978.md) who gave it a more socialist interpretation.[^cite_note-310]

After a brief [grand coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_coalition) between Left and Right during World War II, the center-right parties struggled to cooperate due to their ideological differences: the agrarian populism of the [Centre Party](217370.md), the urban liberalism of the [Liberal People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberals_\(Sweden\)), and the liberal-conservative elitism of the [Moderate Party](202985.md) (the old Conservative Party). However, in [1976](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%A4lldin_I_cabinet) and in [1979](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%A4lldin_II_cabinet), the three parties managed to form a government under [Thorbjörn Fälldin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thorbj%C3%B6rn_F%C3%A4lldin)—and again in [1991](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Bildt_cabinet) under aristocrat [Carl Bildt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Bildt) and with support from the newly founded [Christian Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Democrats_\(Sweden\)), the most conservative party in contemporary Sweden.[^cite_note-311]

In modern times, [mass immigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_immigration) from distant cultures caused a large populist dissatisfaction, which was not channeled through any of the established parties, who generally espoused [multiculturalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiculturalism).[^cite_note-312] Instead, the 2010s saw the rise of the right-wing populist [Sweden Democrats](65873.md), who were surging as the largest party in the polls on several occasions.[^cite_note-313][^cite_note-314] Due to its fascist roots, the party was ostracized by the other parties until 2019 when Christian Democrat leader [Ebba Busch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebba_Busch) reached out for collaboration, after which the Moderate Party followed suit.[^cite_note-315] In [2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Swedish_general_election), the center-right parties formed a [government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kristersson_cabinet) with support from the Sweden Democrats as the largest party.[^cite_note-316] The subsequent [Tidö Agreement](72007581.md), negotiated in [Tidö Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tid%C3%B6_Castle), incorporated authoritarian policies such as a stricter stance on immigration and a harsher stance on law and order.[^cite_note-317]

In some aspects, Swiss conservatism is unique, as Switzerland is an old federal republic born from historically sovereign [cantons](180752.md), comprising three major nationalities and adhering to the principle of [Swiss neutrality](15380880.md).

There are a number of conservative parties in Switzerland's parliament, the [Federal Assembly](1797312.md). These include the largest ones: the [Swiss People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_People's_Party) (SVP),[^cite_note-Hans-Georg-318] the [Christian Democratic People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Democratic_People's_Party_of_Switzerland) (CVP),[^cite_note-Juravich-319] and the [Conservative Democratic Party of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Democratic_Party_of_Switzerland) (BDP),[^cite_note-Schwok-320] which is a splinter of the SVP created in the aftermath to the election of [Eveline Widmer-Schlumpf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eveline_Widmer-Schlumpf) as Federal Council.[^cite_note-Schwok-320]

The SVP was formed from the 1971 merger of the [Party of Farmers, Traders and Citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_of_Farmers,_Traders_and_Independents), formed in 1917, and the smaller Democratic Party, formed in 1942. The SVP emphasized agricultural policy and was strong among farmers in German-speaking Protestant areas. As Switzerland considered closer relations with the European Union in the 1990s, the SVP adopted a more militant [protectionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectionism) and [isolationist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isolationism) stance. This stance has allowed it to expand into German-speaking Catholic mountainous areas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESiaroff2000446-321] The [Anti-Defamation League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Defamation_League), a non-Swiss lobby group based in the United States has accused them of manipulating issues such as immigration, Swiss neutrality, and welfare benefits, awakening antisemitism and racism.[^cite_note-322] The [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe) has called the SVP "[extreme right](221220.md)", although some scholars dispute this classification. For instance, [Hans-Georg Betz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans-Georg_Betz) describes it as "populist radical right".[^cite_note-323] The SVP has been the largest party since 2003.

#### Ukraine

The authoritarian [Ukrainian State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_State) was headed by Cossack aristocrat [Pavlo Skoropadskyi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavlo_Skoropadskyi) and represented the conservative movement. The 1918 [Hetman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hetman_of_Ukraine) government, which appealed to the tradition of the 17th–18th century [Cossack Hetman state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cossack_Hetmanate), represented the conservative strand in Ukraine's struggle for independence. It had the support of the proprietary classes and of conservative and moderate political groups. [Vyacheslav Lypynsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vyacheslav_Lypynsky) was a main ideologue of Ukrainian conservatism.[^cite_note-324]

Modern English conservatives celebrate Anglo-Irish statesman [Edmund Burke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Burke) as their intellectual father. Burke was affiliated with the [Whig Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whigs_\(British_political_party\)), which eventually split among the [Liberal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Party_\(UK\)) and the [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)), but the modern Conservative Party is generally thought to derive primarily from the [Tories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tories_\(British_political_party\)), and the MPs of the modern conservative party are still frequently referred to as Tories.[^cite_note-\:5-325]

Shortly after Burke's death in 1797, conservatism was revived as a mainstream political force as the Whigs suffered a series of internal divisions. This new generation of conservatives derived their politics not from Burke, but from his predecessor, the [Viscount Bolingbroke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_St_John,_1st_Viscount_Bolingbroke), who was a [Jacobite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobitism) and traditional Tory, lacking Burke's sympathies for Whiggish policies such as [Catholic emancipation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_emancipation) and [American independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution) (famously attacked by [Samuel Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Johnson) in "Taxation No Tyranny").[^cite_note-\:5-325]

In the first half of the 19th century, many newspapers, magazines, and journals promoted [loyalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loyalism) or right-wing attitudes in religion, politics, and international affairs. Burke was seldom mentioned, but [William Pitt the Younger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Pitt_the_Younger) became a conspicuous hero. The most prominent journals included the *[Quarterly Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quarterly_Review)*, founded in 1809 as a counterweight to the Whigs' *[Edinburgh Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edinburgh_Review)*, and the even more conservative *[Blackwood's Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackwood's_Magazine)*. The *Quarterly Review* promoted a balanced Canningite Toryism, as it was neutral on Catholic emancipation and only mildly critical of [Nonconformist dissent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonconformist_\(Protestantism\)); it opposed slavery and supported the current poor laws; and it was "aggressively [imperialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_imperialism)". The [high-church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-church) clergy of the Church of England read the *[Orthodox Churchman's Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Churchman's_Magazine)*, which was equally hostile to Jewish, Catholic, [Jacobin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobin), [Methodist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodist) and [Unitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitarianism) spokesmen. Anchoring the [ultra-Tories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultra-Tories), *Blackwood's Edinburgh Magazine* stood firmly against Catholic emancipation and favored slavery, cheap money, [mercantilism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercantilism), the [Navigation Acts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navigation_Acts), and the [Holy Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Alliance).[^cite_note-\:5-325]

Conservatism evolved after 1820, embracing [free trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade) in 1846 and a commitment to democracy, especially under [Benjamin Disraeli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Disraeli). The effect was to significantly strengthen conservatism as a grassroots political force. Conservatism no longer was the philosophical defense of the landed aristocracy, but had been refreshed into redefining its commitment to the ideals of order, both secular and religious, expanding imperialism, strengthened [monarchy](47063.md), and a more generous vision of the welfare state as opposed to the punitive vision of the Whigs and liberals.[^cite_note-326] As early as 1835, Disraeli attacked the Whigs and utilitarians as slavishly devoted to an industrial [oligarchy](22315.md), while he described his fellow Tories as the only "really democratic party of England", devoted to the interests of the whole people.[^cite_note-327] Nevertheless, inside the party there was a tension between the growing numbers of wealthy businessmen on the one side and the aristocracy and rural gentry on the other.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAuerbach195939–40-328] The aristocracy gained strength as businessmen discovered they could use their wealth to buy a peerage and a country estate.

Some conservatives lamented the passing of a pastoral world where the ethos of *[noblesse oblige](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noblesse_oblige)* had promoted respect from the lower classes. They saw the [Anglican Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican_Church) and the aristocracy as balances against commercial wealth.[^cite_note-329] They worked toward legislation for improved working conditions and urban housing.[^cite_note-330] This viewpoint would later be called [Tory democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tory_democracy).[^cite_note-331] However, since Burke, there has always been tension between traditional aristocratic conservatism and the wealthy liberal business class.[^cite_note-332]

In 1834, Tory [Prime Minister](24150.md) [Robert Peel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Peel) issued the "[Tamworth Manifesto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamworth_Manifesto)", in which he pledged to endorse moderate political reform. This marked the beginning of the transformation from [High Tory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Tory) reactionism towards a more modern form of conservatism. As a result, the party became known as the [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\))—a name it has retained to this day. However, Peel would also be the root of a split in the party between the traditional Tories (by the [Earl of Derby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Smith-Stanley,_14th_Earl_of_Derby) and [Benjamin Disraeli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Disraeli)) and the "Peelites" (led first by Peel himself, then by the [Earl of Aberdeen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Hamilton-Gordon,_4th_Earl_of_Aberdeen)). The split occurred in 1846 over the issue of [free trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade), which Peel supported, versus [protectionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectionism), supported by Derby. The majority of the party sided with Derby while about a third split away, eventually merging with the [Whigs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whigs_\(British_political_party\)) and the [radicals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radicalism_\(politics\)) to form the [Liberal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Party_\(UK\)). Despite the split, the mainstream Conservative Party accepted the doctrine of free trade in 1852.

In the second half of the 19th century, the Liberal Party faced political schisms, especially over [Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Ireland) [Home Rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Parliamentary_Party). Leader [William Gladstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Ewart_Gladstone) (himself a former Peelite) sought to give Ireland a degree of autonomy, a move that elements in both the left and right-wings of his party opposed. These split off to become the [Liberal Unionists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Unionist_Party) (led by [Joseph Chamberlain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Chamberlain)), forming a coalition with the Conservatives before merging with them in 1912. The Liberal Unionist influence dragged the Conservative Party towards the left as Conservative governments passed a number of progressive reforms at the turn of the 20th century. By the late 19th century, the traditional business supporters of the Liberal Party had joined the Conservatives, making them the party of business and commerce as well.

After a period of Liberal dominance before World War I, the Conservatives gradually became more influential in government, regaining full control of the cabinet in 1922. In the inter-war period, conservatism was the major ideology in Britain[^cite_note-333][^cite_note-334][^cite_note-335] as the Liberal Party vied with the [Labour Party](19279158.md) for control of the left. After World War II, the first Labour government (1945–1951) under [Clement Attlee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clement_Attlee) embarked on a program of nationalization of industry and the promotion of social welfare. The Conservatives generally accepted those policies until the 1980s.

In the 1980s, the Conservative government of [Margaret Thatcher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher), guided by [neoliberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoliberalism) economics, reversed many of Labour's social programmes, privatized large parts of the UK economy, and sold state-owned assets.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcLeanMcMillan2009364-336] The Conservative Party also adopted [soft eurosceptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft_euroscepticism) politics and opposed [Federal Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Europe). Other conservative political parties, such as the [Democratic Unionist Party](221428.md) (DUP, founded in 1971), and the [United Kingdom Independence Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_Independence_Party) (UKIP, founded in 1993), began to appear, although they have yet to make any significant impact at Westminster. As of 2014, the DUP is the largest political party in the ruling coalition in the [Northern Ireland Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Ireland_Assembly), and from 2017 to 2019 the DUP provided support for the Conservative [minority government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_May_ministry) under a confidence-and-supply arrangement.

### Latin America

Main article: [Conservatism in Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_Latin_America)

Conservative elites have long dominated Latin American nations. Mostly, this has been achieved through control of civil institutions, the Catholic Church, and the military, rather than through party politics. Typically, the Church was exempt from taxes and its employees immune from civil prosecution. Where conservative parties were weak or non-existent, conservatives were more likely to rely on [military dictatorship](146728.md) as a preferred form of government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERemmer1989-337]

However, in some nations where the elites were able to mobilize popular support for conservative parties, longer periods of political stability were achieved. Chile, Colombia, and Venezuela are examples of nations that developed strong conservative parties. Argentina, Brazil, El Salvador, and Peru are examples of nations where this did not occur.[^cite_note-338]

Political scientist [Louis Hartz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Hartz) explained conservatism in Latin American nations as a result of their settlement as feudal societies.[^cite_note-339]

Conservatism in Brazil originates from the cultural and historical tradition of Brazil, whose cultural roots are [Luso](23033.md)-[Iberian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula) and [Roman Catholic](606848.md).[^cite_note-340] More traditional conservative historical views and features include belief in political [federalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalism) and [monarchism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchism_in_Brazil). Brazil is the only Latin American nation with a relatively strong royalist sentiment, and throughout modern history a significant minority of the population has always supported a monarchical restoration.[^cite_note-\:12-77][^cite_note-341]

The [military dictatorship in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_dictatorship_in_Brazil) was established on April 1, 1964, after a *coup d'état* by the [Brazilian Army](1047559.md) with support from the United States government, and it lasted for 21 years, until March 15, 1985. The coup received support from almost all high-ranking members of the military along with conservative sectors in society, such as the Catholic Church and anti-communist civilian movements among the Brazilian middle and upper classes. The dictatorship reached the height of its popularity in the 1970s with the so-called [Brazilian Miracle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Miracle). Brazil's military government provided a model for other military regimes throughout [Latin America](18524.md), being systematized by the "National Security Doctrine", which was used to justify the military's actions as operating in the interest of national security in a time of crisis.[^cite_note-342]

In contemporary politics, a [conservative wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_wave#Brazil) began roughly around the [2014 Brazilian presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Brazilian_presidential_election).[^cite_note-343] According to commentators, the [National Congress of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Congress_of_Brazil) elected in 2014 may be considered the most conservative since the re-democratization movement, citing an increase in the number of parliamentarians linked to more conservative segments, such as [ruralists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruralists), [the military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_of_Brazil), [the police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_of_Brazil), and [religious conservatives](#Religious_conservatism). The subsequent economic crisis of 2015 and investigations of corruption scandals led to a right-wing movement that sought to rescue ideas from capitalism in opposition to socialism. At the same time, fiscal conservatives such as those that make up the [Free Brazil Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Brazil_Movement) emerged among many others. Military officer [Jair Bolsonaro](46603496.md) of the [Social Liberal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Liberal_Party_\(Brazil\)) was the winner of the [2018 Brazilian presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Brazilian_presidential_election).[^cite_note-344]

The [Chilean Civil War of 1829-1830](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chilean_Civil_War_of_1829-1830) was a [civil war](7085.md) in Chile between conservative [Pelucones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pelucones) and liberal [Pipiolos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pipiolos) forces over the constitutional system. Pelucones won the civil war, leading to the [Conservative Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Republic) from 1829 to 1861.

Chile's conservative party, the [National Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Party_\(Chile,_1966%E2%80%931973\)), disbanded in 1973 following a military coup and did not re-emerge as a political force after the return to democracy.[^cite_note-345] During the [military dictatorship of Chile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_dictatorship_of_Chile), the country was ruled by a [military junta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_Junta_of_Chile_\(1973\)) headed by General [Augusto Pinochet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augusto_Pinochet). His ideology, known as [Pinochetism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinochetism), was anti-communist, militaristic, nationalistic, and *laissez-faire* capitalistic.[^cite_note-346] Under Pinochet, [Chile's economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chile's_economy) was placed under the control of a group of economists known collectively as the [Chicago Boys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Boys), whose [liberalizing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalization) policies have been described as [neoliberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoliberalism).[^cite_note-347]
